It is well known to all what great results a constant belief <note>original: "Credulity"</note> in an elevated or raised mind can achieve through a strong imagination—so great, even, that in intense false opinions and operations, it works miracles. But hesitation of belief and lack of confidence <note>original: "diffidence"</note> do not only shatter and break the virtue or force of the operating mind (which is the middle ground between both extremes), but they also weaken every work, in superstition as well as in true religion, and weaken <note>original: "enervate"</note> the desired effect of even the most powerful experiments.

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;All things are possible to those who believe and are willing; all things are impossible to the unbeliever and the rebellious. As a person thinks and imagines a thing to be, so it must be. Unbelief is the greatest and most powerful enemy of faith, for the imagination joined with faith can do all things. Fate <note>original: "The Fates"</note> also makes diseases incurable, which we perceive by the denial of the help we expected from the administering of proper medicines.

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;And as must be diligently observed here, our Savior (as Holy Scripture says) was not able to perform miracles in Capernaum because they did not believe, but resisted him with their incredulity and false faith. For just as man can do nothing without God, so likewise God will do nothing without man as his instrument <note>original: "Organ or Instrument"</note>; God and the creature work together, neither one acting without the other. Therefore, no man ought to will anything without God, because no man can exist <note>original: "Be"</note> without God, in whom we are, live, and move.

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Eighthly <note>original: "Eighty," likely the eighth point in a list of reasons why healing may fail</note>, lest the sick person, upon recovering, should commit greater sins against God, his neighbor, and himself to the loss of his soul.

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the spiritual causes of illness and the role of divine providence in suffering. The previous page likely established the connection between sin and physical ailment.</meta>

->[ 177 ]<-

<margin>Job 33:19, 20</margin>
For every sickness is, as it were, an expiation <note>original: expiation (the act of making amends or reparation for guilt)</note> or a propitiatory sacrifice <note>original: propitiatory (intended to appease or reconcile)</note>. Either the just Judge—through this divine, innate retribution <note>original: revenge</note>, penance, and scourge—calls the person to amend their life for the future, or else by this fatherly visitation and the laying on of the cross (which he ought to endure patiently), he may become an example to himself and his neighbor, leading to a more fervent fear and worship of God. For God often permits some men to fall into many great illnesses—men whose minds have been grievously infected and disturbed by the pleasant health of the flesh and the continuation of their sins—resulting in the loss of their eternal health. For health is worth nothing—being nothing but condemnation to us—if our sins are not forgiven. Moreover, sins weaken the powers of the soul and make it incapable of the natural government of the body; therefore, the powers of the body are enfeebled and hasten toward death. Otherwise, the sick person is held under this yoke and bridle of infirmity <note>original: infirmity (physical weakness or illness)</note> as if by a wholesome purgatory <note>original: purgatory (a place or state of suffering for purification)</note>—though few are actually reformed by their sickness—at least leading them toward the duty of piety while that loose licentiousness of sinning is lopped and cut off, which the person would have abused if they were to live and remain healthy any longer.

<margin>A physician begins by appeasing God's wrath. Job 33:26.</margin>
In these diseases that arise from the resisting astral <note>original: Astrall (relating to the stars or celestial influence on the body)</note> impression and the wrath of God, no physical <note>original: Corporall</note> medicine can do any good (because God has shut the doors of Nature), but only celestial and spiritual remedies—namely, through serious repentance and heartily bewailing the sins that we have [committed]...

<summary>This page explores the theological purpose of illness, framing sickness as a divine tool for penance, spiritual correction, and the prevention of further sin. It argues that physical medicine is useless for diseases caused by divine wrath, requiring instead spiritual repentance.</summary>
<keywords>divine providence, sickness as penance, expiation, astral influence, spiritual healing, repentance, Job 33, soul and body connection</keywords>

<lang>English</lang>

...committed, to soothe the provoked wrath of God by amending our lives and reconciling with our neighbors; to be reconciled to God through our Savior—the celestial physician of our souls—and to submit our will to the most righteous pleasure of the divine will, devoutly, patiently, and humbly in all things, with a most assured participation in divine mercy. *Paracelsus* <note>Philippus Aureolus Theophrastus Bombastus von Hohenheim (1493–1541), a Swiss physician and alchemist who integrated spiritual and physical medicine.</note> calls them divine diseases <note>original: "Deal diseases," likely derived from the Latin *dealis*, meaning divine.</note>, which come from the *Ens* <term>Ens</term> <note>Latin for "entity" or "essence." In Paracelsian philosophy, the *Ens Dei* (Essence of God) is one of the five influences that can cause disease.</note> of God, who inflicts all such diseases and who alone works through both good and bad men. The cure, he says, is through faith; or, once the purging <note>the process of spiritual or physical cleansing</note> is perfected, the physician may then try his skill. The causes of those diseases that arise from the essence of God are unsearchable; in these cases, the cure must be sought through faith, not in nature. Likewise, in the healing of divine diseases that come from the essence of God—whether through a deific <note>god-like or divine</note> or faithful cure—the predestined term or period, according to divine pleasure, must be observed.

## CHAP. VI.

### Of that one only chief Medicine of the most Ancient Philosophers.

<margin>This hidden <term>Minerva</term><note>The Roman goddess of wisdom; used here to represent the "hidden" wisdom of alchemy.</note> of philosophy and this uniquely precious jewel is beyond price.</margin>
<margin>( ! )</margin>
**F**urthermore, I shall provide here what still pertains to that chief and universal philosophical medicine <note>original: "Phisick"</note>. We never read of a greater gift of wisdom bestowed on man by divine bounty than this. This even includes the "Rational Soul" <note>original: "Reasonable Soul"; in the philosophy of the time, this was the part of the soul unique to humans that allows for reasoning and intellect.</note> itself; next to God in Heaven and Earth, the soul cannot contemplate or discover anything bestowed upon man more admirable, more sublime, more noble, or more excellent than this <gloss>most secret Secret of secret Secrets</gloss>. By this, wonders—indeed, all things—may be accomplished. This applies to the planets of <term>inferior astronomy</term><note>A common alchemical term for alchemy itself, where metals are viewed as earthly reflections of the planets.</note>, whose imperfections and <term>dross</term><note>Impure matter or waste.</note> it causes to vanish by piercing them with its most powerful influence. It separates all external <term>sulphureity</term><note>The alchemical principle of "sulfur," representing combustibility and soul.</note> and impure <term>terrestreity</term><note>The alchemical principle of "earth," representing solid matter and impurity.</note> from metallic and human bodies. It also serves to recover and preserve the fading and lost health of the human body through its fiery vigor, besides performing infinite other things; to say no-

<summary>This page introduces the "chief medicine" of the ancient philosophers, describing it as a divine gift capable of purifying both metals and the human body. The author frames this alchemical secret as the most noble discovery available to the human soul, possessing the power to remove impurities and restore health.</summary>

<keywords>philosopher's stone, universal medicine, alchemy, inferior astronomy, rational soul, sulfur, purification, transmutation</keywords>

<margin>See the Monad of **John Dee** of London, and **Roger Bacon.** <note>John Dee (1527–1608/9) was a famous mathematician and occultist; his "Monas Hieroglyphica" explains a symbol representing the unity of the cosmos. Roger Bacon was a 13th-century friar and philosopher associated with early science and alchemy.</note></margin>
...something now of the magical and super-celestial <note>higher than the heavens</note> use: when the generative <note>original: "gonetick," referring to procreative or reproductive power</note> influence of the beams of the Sun and Moon and the fourth revolution is finished upon its native earth, it is endowed absolutely with all created influential power. This power extends as well to the elemental world as to the celestial or super-celestial; it is the most wonderful of all wonders. For as God is wonderful in all his works, so does he usually hide his wonderful gifts in wonderful men. All antiquity, as well as the universal truths of all nations and languages in the tradition of this doctrine, bear witness that this is so. This is further confirmed by the consensus of all those most learned men who, in every age, have lived with the greatest admiration and praise of many. Moreover, besides the eyewitness observation and certification of many in our own time, it is easy to determine this truth from their writings, which are woven with so many **hieroglyphical**, **magical**, and **mathematical** coverings in such a great and certain series of **philosophical truth**.

Who then would not admire and embrace so great a gift from the greatest God—the immortal prize for one's study, pains, and virtue? To the pious and holy <term>philosophers</term> <note>In this context, "philosopher" refers specifically to alchemists or seekers of the "Philosopher's Stone."</note> dignified by nature and education, this gift warrants and assures the removal of old age and the renewal of youth, perpetual health, and honest food and clothing <note>original: "rayment"</note>. This is achieved without hindrance to our neighbor—not by usury and fraud, nor by selling fraudulent goods, nor by the oppression of the poor (as most of our rich men are enriched today)—but by the industry of work and the labor of one's own hands.

<meta>This page continues the author's defense of his work, following his promise to share practical findings. The catchword "igno-" at the bottom suggests the next page begins with a word like "ignorant" or "ignorance."</meta>

-> [ 181 ] <-

God forbid, therefore, that by slighting the example of the Ancients, I should either deny such precious wonders of GOD or obscure the wonderful powers of Nature. For he who despises knowledge is himself despised by the glorious and high God for rejecting this most true <term>Art</term> <note>original: "Art"; here referring to the sacred practice of alchemy or natural philosophy</note>. Nor would I revile these wonders as most people do, as if they were merely the speculations of idle men or the empty dreams and fictions of a foolish and doting mind. Those who do so only betray the weakness of their own judgment to wise men and openly testify to their own folly. Therefore, those who revile and are ignorant of these divine banquets—whom the <term>Philosophers</term> call fools—are not to be admitted to them; likewise, those who wander in their own fantastical dreams are to be utterly excluded.

Here some may be listening to hear whether, trusting to my own ingenuity, I dare also boast of the preparation of this work in this place, or whether for the sake of show I ambitiously claim for myself the absolute knowledge of this Art, as those greedy <term>mountebanks</term> <note>original: "Mountebanks"; charlatans or quacks who deceive the public for money</note> and greedy <term>philosophists</term> <note>original: "Phylosophists"; pseudo-philosophers or those who practice a shallow, deceptive version of philosophy</note> are accustomed to do.

<margin>Here I would be regarded as an index <note>original: "Index"; a pointer or signpost</note>, or as one standing at the door to direct others the way they should go, whom I may benefit more than myself; or as a whetstone to encourage and sharpen them.</margin>

But because I promised the courteous reader a little while ago that I would set down at least those things which I had personally tested, I was unwilling in this place to openly and wickedly falsify anything concerning the certain truth of this matter—which is not the part of a wise man, but of cheaters.

For this sacred and divine <term>Art</term> and <term>Science</term>, belonging not to <term>sophisters</term> <note>original: "Sophisters"; those who use clever but fallacious and deceptive arguments</note> but to <term>philosophers</term>, which the

...which the ignorant basely and wretchedly condemn as false. Undoubtedly, among all the arts—both the <term>liberal arts</term> <note>original: "Liberall"; the traditional academic subjects such as grammar, logic, and rhetoric</note> and the <term>mechanical arts</term> <note>original: "Mechanick"; crafts involving manual labor or technical skill</note>—none are as full of impostors as this one. Yet, it deserves to be reverenced for the wonderful secrets it contains, and it should be preferred above all other earthly sciences by those who are true <term>physicians</term> <note>original: "Physitians"; here meaning practitioners of natural medicine and philosophy</note>. Such men, being enlightened with the spirit of <term>divine wisdom</term>, are content and provided with honest food and clothing; for it is impossible for a poor or greedy man to spend his time in the study of <term>philosophy</term>. Following the example of Solomon, they pray to God with religious veneration—not for wealth, but for wisdom—so that He might open to them the <term>storehouse of divine knowledge</term> <note>original: "Magazine of Divine knowledge"</note>.

These are people who measure their happiness by the standards of Heaven and the love of God, who is the giver of every good thing.

They are also moved and spurred on to search out these truths by a love of secrets and of nature, according to <term>divine grace</term>.

<margin>By wisdom the heart is made constant.</margin>

Furthermore, through a desire to gain knowledge—without any foolish hope for profit or a craving for empty glory—they do not refuse any honest, steady, or practical labor of their hands in the fear of God.

And lastly, they are those who have no malicious intentions and ignore the spring of dry human greed. They desire most humbly, and with fear and trembling, to use such great gifts for the purpose intended by the Master of Nature: namely, the praise and honor of God, and the good of his

By these means, I say, among the "Children of the Golden Doctrine" <note>This likely refers to alchemists or those following a divine philosophy where spiritual gold is the ultimate goal.</note> (whose gold is the Almighty God), that most rare Good—which is to be preferred above all riches—is justly and rightly sought after for the sake of human health. These seekers leave behind all other business and employment. They leave the pursuit of common metals to those who—driven by an unholy hunger and a thirsty, insatiable desire to be rich—make no distinction between right and wrong. Such people horribly vex and torment themselves day and night, to the great hazard of both body and soul.

A philosopher must covet nothing but wisdom, which is concerned with divine things. Therefore, a true philosopher never sought after nor desired riches, but is instead delighted with the mysteries of nature. For truly, he who is an <term>adept</term> <note>One who has achieved the highest level of knowledge in alchemy or esoteric philosophy.</note>, or has attained this wisdom, may carry with him in his purse not just the worth of a single kingdom, but the wealth of almost the whole world. In God and with God, he possesses as a master all things of the entire world, and in the fear and service of God, he commands the whole of creation. But that gift is acquired neither by anger nor by forced violence; it comes either through the immediate inspiration of the Most High, or by the expert visual demonstration <note>original: ocular demonstration</note> of a wise master in this art.

That this is so beyond all controversy, no man who applies himself to philosophy...

And although perhaps <note>original: "happily"</note> some person or other may obtain the key to the *<term>Philosophical Garden</term>* (as I know some who have), yet because the gate was bolted—that is, Divine Grace and blessing were denied and stood in the way—they could never open it, much less enter and gather those desired fruits of the *<term>Hermetic Tree</term>*, or eat the most sweet kernels of this mystery. Similarly, some impostors in our age, who by right or wrong through unlawful means have obtained the most true *<term>Philosophical Ferment</term>* or *<term>Leaven</term>* <note>The "ferment" or "leaven" refers to the substance used to transform base metals into gold or to catalyze alchemical reactions</note> (though prepared by others), have not been able to improve or multiply it any further because of their ignorance of its fundamental principle. This is the work that is hidden under the robe of the *<term>Philosophical Virgin</term>*, which even one brother would not teach another; and therefore it is useless to tempt an Adept Philosopher <note>An Adept is a practitioner who has successfully achieved the "Great Work" of alchemy</note> with promises of rewards, favors, or any other kind of consideration imaginable to convince them to part with it.

[Out of fear of] God, and the curses <note>original: "execration"</note> of those **Sages** <note>original: "Sophie's," from the Greek "Sophoi" meaning wise men</note> or **wise men** which they leave to their descendants, [these practitioners] study and strive to be harsh and difficult, lest they should disclose their more hidden or obscure meaning to anyone, and expose the most noble **gem** of this **science** <note>Here, "science" refers specifically to the art of alchemy</note> to "hogs and swine." Through a wise and clever craft, they have covenanted and sworn that they would never write of it plainly in any **book**. Nor has any of them, who kept this knowledge in the secret of their heart, made it known to anyone except those who had an allegorical understanding. 

For undoubtedly, that liberty is granted to the **philosophers**, that because they are made **masters of things**, they should also have the power to dispense or give **names** at their pleasure, and "clothe their children" <note>A metaphor for how they name their chemical substances or processes</note> as they saw fit. <margin>This is not believed at first, but once it is proven through labor, toil, and risk, it becomes clear toward the end, which proves the success of the work.</margin> Even though **true philosophers** have accurately and with the greatest ingenuity mutually committed themselves to one and the same thing, those who "till the field" <note>An alchemical metaphor for those who perform the actual laboratory work</note> have always observed it. Certainly, in their instructions—as if looking into a mirror—it has been sufficiently revealed to men of wise understanding who are chosen by **God** for such mysteries: worthy men, sworn into this **Art**.

<meta>This page continues the author's critique of fraudulent alchemists and "smoke-sellers" who deceive the public with false promises of wealth and transmutation.</meta>

...and frantic inventions, enrich the ears of credulous men so that they may make their pockets poor. They promise great matters and pretend <note>original: "faine"</note> that they can do all things, but their unwary disciples get nothing from them except—after being cheated three or four times—to be deceived again by fresh and most subtle juggling, deceit, and fraud. We should avoid their company even as we avoid the most raging torments of hell.

<margin>
Great gain is the goal <note>original: "scope"</note> of sophisticated craft, because dust and ashes are the end for such sellers of smoke <note>Smoke-sellers: a term for those who sell false promises or non-existent things.</note>.
</margin>

And what is the most important thing of all, the Wisdom of the Lord—whose mystery is only with those who fear Him—does not enter into such wicked and unworthy souls. These are people who have spent the whole course of their lives searching out vanities and deceits, and who have maliciously and intentionally deceived almost the entire world with their deceitful devices for painting white and red and plastering <note>original: "pargetting"; usually refers to plastering walls, used here to describe the heavy application of makeup</note> women's faces.

Thus, these men gamble away and lose the story of *Pandora* <note>In alchemical literature, Pandora's Box was sometimes used as a metaphor for the vessel containing the "mystery" of the Stone.</note>. In the end, nothing falls to their share except what *Alphidius* <note>An Arabic alchemical authority frequently cited in the Middle Ages and Renaissance.</note> foretold would happen to them: that when their brains grow giddy, instead of the Tincture <note>The "Elixir" or "Philosopher's Stone" in liquid form, capable of transmuting base metals into gold.</note> they should find only the color; instead of the Hermetic Stone <note>The Philosopher's Stone.</note>, a pebble <note>original: "pibble"</note> or glass; and instead of great treasure and riches, nothing but coals and ashes.

<margin>
Piety is the most necessary key to the knowledge of all secrets. See the verse of Rodargitus Britto Sarmatus in *The Zodiac of Pisces* <note>original: *Zodiaco Piscium*</note>.
</margin>

And who would not marvel at this "effective" transmuting power of these impostors, which makes wise men fools, strong men weak, rich men poor, and bankrupt poor men into desperate, wandering beggars?

<meta>This page continues the author's personal testimony regarding the miraculous healing powers of a specific alchemical substance, following a discussion on the benefits of these secrets for future generations.</meta>

For the happiness of posterity, I cannot help but remember here—as a witness and confirmation of the truth—the extraordinary divine mercy shown to me. This was granted through the clear favor of God while I was traveling, a privilege denied to many others who earnestly desired it: that I should see and taste this substance at the house of a great man, *“May it be well with him forever, and especially the excellent Helioch Anthar of the North, who now rests in Christ; such as have finally been accustomed to lie hidden in the slow-moving cycles of time.”* <note>original: "Cui in æternum bene sit, & Cumprimis egregium Helioch Antharum borEaLem, nunc in Chriſto quieſcentem : cujusmodi lENtis DenIque conſueVerunt lati- tare tempoRum currIcULis." The unusual capitalization in the original Latin is a chronogram, where specific letters represent Roman numerals to encode a date.</note> I will refrain from translating the rest into English.

<margin>
The **Philosophical Basilisk** <note>a term for a powerful alchemical agent or "philosopher's stone" in its active state</note>, like lightning, suddenly and unexpectedly burns up any imperfect metal whatsoever, and instantly produces another new form. Therefore, searching for it is most highly recommended to all who study the truth.
</margin>

Because of this, I was for a long time astonished and amazed at the greatest miracle of Nature produced through Art <note>meaning "skill" or "alchemy"</note>. Among the various and manifold transformations <note>original: "metamorphosis"</note> of "Inferior Astronomy" <note>a term for alchemy, which studies the "stars" or virtues hidden within earthly metals</note> performed in the cold (the "Moist Way" of the ancients not yet being intensely raised to the "eye of the Basilisk"), this one wonder seemed strange and most worthy to be seen. 

By giving only a single drop of that liquid <note>original: "Latex"</note>—which acted as a storehouse where the dispersed virtues of all celestial and terrestrial bodies were invisibly gathered together in a heap by a wonderful skill (indeed, the whole world was centered within it)—a man desperately sick and at the point of death was recovered. He was healed by its fiery, astral, and celestial invisible nature, which conveyed a beam of natural life to the heart, renewing the vital organs and repairing his constitution <note>original: "Nature"</note>, which was spent (due to an accidental illness causing its removal)...

Surely this entire newly regenerated world is able, by virtue of its regeneration, to renew the "little world" of man—the old, corruptible <term>Microcosm</term> <note>The "small world"; the idea that a human being is a miniature reflection of the entire universe.</note>—to restore whatever is wrong in man's body, to consume what is superfluous, to mend what is defective, and to bring the whole microcosm into a true balance <note>original: "Temperament"; the proper proportions of the four humors or elements.</note>, preserving it there until the appointed time of death imposed on mortals because of their sin.

Imperfect and impure metals are cleansed of their infirmities and <term>accidents</term> <note>In Aristotelian philosophy, "accidents" are non-essential qualities (like color or smell) that can change without changing the essential nature of the substance.</note> by the same spirit of the world and the same heat of the Sun and Moon that heal human bodies. They are restored to their true health, which is <term>aureity</term> <note>The essential quality or state of being gold.</note> or goldenness. This happens without a new process of creation and decay <note>original: "generation and corruption"</note>, but only through alteration and the removal of the accidents which are the cause of the "sickness" and imbalance of the metal. For metals do not differ in their basic species <note>The author argues that all metals are essentially the same substance at different stages of purity.</note>, but only in their accidents.

<meta>This page continues a discussion on the universal medicine (the Philosopher's Stone) and the divine grace required to achieve the "Great Work" of alchemy. The catchword from the previous page, "rofore," suggests the opening sentence begins with "Wherefore" or "Therefore."</meta>

<margin>It is not the first birth, but the second, that produces it.</margin>
[Wherefore, whatever the] cures may be, they are healed by this universal medicine—just as a carpenter squares all kinds of timber—unless the person has reached the final limit appointed to him by the Supreme Being, or the disease is inflicted by God, beyond the laws of Nature, as a punishment and special affliction.

But no one, as has been said, can make use of this unique and celestial gift except for the person on whom God Himself has bestowed it. He alone both illuminates what is obscure and darkens the clarity of these mysteries, so that no one can understand even the simplest things unless He enlightens them, nor can anyone remain ignorant of the darkest secrets if He illustrates them; for such a great faculty is granted only by the rich and unique grace of the most high Creator.

Therefore *Lullius* <note>original: "Lullius"; referring to Ramon Llull (c. 1232–1315), a Majorcan philosopher whose works were highly influential in later alchemical traditions.</note>, that divine and most perfect philosopher, rightly concluded that between the artist <note>original: "Artist"; the practitioner of alchemy or the "Hermetic Art."</note> and God, the First Cause, there ought to be an agreement without conflict. He believed that the First Mover, as the principal Form, should move the intellect or soul of the artist toward a true understanding, so that He may reveal to him what is hidden in the mastery <note>original: "magistery"; the technical process or the successful completion of the alchemist's "Great Work."</note> of this art. Blessed is the person whom the Lord God is pleased to inspire with the gifts of His grace; for it is the Lord of Heaven who knows the hearts of those through whom He would exercise form and measure. Nevertheless, we see men sometimes offend not only against God through their ingratitude, but also against their innocent neighbors through strange schemes unbecoming of an adept <note>original: "Aedpt"; an "adept" is one who has mastered the secrets of alchemy.</note> philosopher—schemes with which some eminent men in the past...

<summary>This page argues that the "universal medicine" can heal all natural ailments unless God decrees otherwise, and emphasizes that the secrets of alchemy can only be understood through divine grace and a harmonious relationship with God.</summary>

--- Page 23 ---
<meta>The previous page concludes with the author preparing to discuss the consequences of revealing philosophical secrets, ending with the catchword "I say."</meta>

<page-num>200</page-num>

Previously, two public philosophers from different nations in our time, who abused the gifts of God by violating those most dreadful *anathemas* <note>original: Anathema's; here meaning sacred prohibitions or curses against revealing secrets</note> of philosophers. Afterward—as undoubtedly every man, according to the skill which the sidereal spirit <note>original: sydereall spirit; the celestial or astral influence on the human mind according to Hermetic thought</note> causes and excites, is the maker of his own fortune—they came to a mournful and lamentable end. This brought perpetual reproach to their names, fitting for their unworthy publication of this most true philosophical art. It was miserably wasted and restrained by the wrath of **GOD**, the righteous revenger, both for their arrogant pride—punishment and repentance following their provocative talkativeness—as also for their cheating deceptions regarding the "Harpocratical" silence <note>original: Hapocraticall silence; referring to Harpocrates, the Greek god of silence and secrecy</note>, which they used by turns to hide the matter for their own safety.

<margin>The origin of the philosophical magistery <note>original: Magistery; the highest aim of alchemy, often referring to the Philosopher's Stone or the completion of the Great Work</note>. Most people act differently now, boasting to others that they have clear knowledge of it when it is only imaginary. They hinder themselves through their own gullibility and convictions, so that they cannot progress or learn anything further.</margin>

Those more ancient and skillful philosophers, who were born under a lucky star <note>original: born in a happy signe</note>, were the "children of *Hermes*" <note>referring to Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary founder of Hermeticism and alchemy</note> who first discovered the science. Among them, nothing was more ancient than Truth, and nothing was more filthy and abominable than falsehood and deceit. They judged it a more worthwhile labor to *actually possess* the knowledge without the witness of a foolish, ignorant crowd, than merely to *seem* or be supposed to have it. They also endeavored to leave behind an unspotted memory for posterity—not as many overly gullible people imagine, thinking that because they were deceived by their own vain imaginations, they would want to deceive others in the same way. Such deception is not the character of an honest man.

<summary>The author warns against the arrogant or public disclosure of alchemical secrets, citing the tragic ends of two contemporary philosophers as divine punishment, while praising the integrity and secrecy of the ancient "children of Hermes."</summary>

<margin>In the first age of the world, God made truth known through the Light of Nature.</margin>
I say, these private (not public) secretaries of nature, who have the **Natural Light** fresh and flourishing in them, followed Reason as the best guide according to the ability God gave them. All of them, with the greatest attention of both body and mind, pressed chiefly toward one and the same goal and scope: **Virtue**. They accounted nothing more glorious than that they might peaceably rejoice, prudently and in **Quiet Silence**, with a *mind truly sound* in a *sound body* <note>original Latin: "mens sana in corpore sano"</note>, according to the **Fear of God** and **Love** of their **neighbor**.

This is **Adept Philosophy** <note>original: "Phylosophy Adept"</note>, which *Paracelsus*—in his work the **Tincture of Physicists** <note>original: "Tincture of Physicks," referring to Paracelsus's "De Tinctura Physicorum"</note>—explains results in a **Long Life** free from all infirmity, even until a **Natural death**. It provides an honest support for that long life in this **vale** <note>original: "vaſe," likely a printing error for "vale," a common metaphor for the world as a place of sorrow</note> of miseries, so that we might serve **God** without poverty or doing harm to our **neighbor**.

Though there may be many hunting after this happiness with a kind of great and continued greediness of mind, they have been persuaded that they should never attain it by any other means or **Arts**, except by a wonderful and most hidden <note>original: "abstruse"</note> comprehension of all the virtues of the whole Creation flowing and running together into one certain mass. This is to be accomplished through this **Road** <note>original: "Rode"</note> or **King's Highway** and **Philosophical Reason**.

<margin>The defects of Nature must be supplied by the industry of Art.</margin>
All these spiritual virtues and active qualities are, by the **great help** of ingenuity and **Art**, heaped up together and concentrated into one mass like a microcosm <note>original: "lesser world"</note> (as united force is stronger than when dispersed).

<lang>English</lang>

<margin>The celestial spirit (the spirit of the world) and the natural spirit of our body are one and the same. Therefore, the heat of the sun and moon, generated by the very action of that spirit, is more refined <note>original: "concoct"</note> and consequently more perfect.</margin>
...or is less restrained by matter. These things work more quickly and perfectly, and they more readily produce their own kind. What is even easier is that the spirit, or that heavenly <note>original: "supernall"</note> fire, is extracted from them by art <note>here meaning alchemical or chemical skill</note>. To this fire belongs the light that gives life to all things and the heat that does not burn like elemental fire, but instead makes all things fruitful. However, a burning heat that consumes everything and a darkness that makes everything barren are characteristics of elemental and inferior fire.

That specific burning heat is therefore excluded, as are all diverse and contrary things, such as the inferior elements. For these elements, and everything else that has a natural composition (as long as they are still "drowned" in thick, coarse matter and not yet separated from it), are subject to decay <note>original: "corruption"</note> and change <note>original: "transmutation"</note>. But medicines ought to be preservative, very durable, and far removed from decay. Because they are meant to protect the human body from decay, they must first be of a long-lasting nature; otherwise, they would cause corruption rather than prevent it.

<margin>Adding one thing to something like itself makes it even more like that thing.</margin>
Besides, it is useless to think one can preserve a decaying <note>original: "corruptible"</note> body with a putrid and decaying substance, or to cure the weak with something feeble, or to shape a thing using something prone to deformity. Every decaying, weak, and feeble thing added to something like itself only increases those qualities. Thus, that tendency toward decay is increased rather than diminished. We see this in some—and truly too many—physicians of our time, who labor in vain to cure people of their illnesses...

...were it not for the wages of sin <note>A reference to the biblical phrase "the wages of sin is death" (Romans 6:23)</note>, it would withstand death, preserve the body in perpetual health, and defend it from destruction.

<margin>That our children in the tenth generation may see the effect of it by living so long. *Paracelsus*</margin>
*Paracelsus* <note>Philippus Aureolus Theophrastus Bombastus von Hohenheim (1493–1541), a Swiss physician and alchemist who pioneered the use of chemicals and minerals in medicine</note> calls it the **Element of FIRE**, which, like the Sun of the Earthly or inferior Firmament, may be the greatest Secret for removing all diseases and refreshing cold, numbed limbs. This is because that Essential Fire works inside the body just as flame and nettles do outside the body. His meaning—so that he may be rightly defended here from the unjust slander with which he was branded—was this: where he treats the virtues of this perfect **Fire of Life**, he intends that the **Balsam of Nature**, the **Balsamic Mummy** <note>original: "Balsamick Mummy"; in Paracelsian medicine, this refers to an internal life-force or "inner balm" that protects the body from decay</note>, the **Vital Body**, the liquor of Life, and the **Native or Radical Humor** <note>original: "Radicall Humor"; the fundamental moisture of the body believed to sustain the vital heat and life force</note> which the **Spirit of Life** moves or acts upon, may be restored, strengthened, and preserved as if uncorrupted.

<margin>The Humors of life nourish the spirit of life. See *Paracelsus* in the first Volume among his medical fragments, folio 162.</margin>
This preservation continues even to the very final consumption of that humor—that is, to the last gasp of Life—without any sickness, pain, or grief. Although he performed this by curing the most desperate and dangerous diseases of other men, he was stopped from continuing to do so any longer. 

<margin>Cease, therefore, from now on to blame *Paracelsus* because he promised long life to others but barely reached middle age himself. They devise and spread many more such insults out of their malicious ignorance, things which *Paracelsus* never thought of.</margin>
He was poisoned in his body by his malicious and inhuman adversaries, who had often attempted the same before they could succeed. For he came to the natural end of his life by an untimely and violent death from a dose of poison—not, as many maliciously mock in their strange fictions, because he intended by this medicine to presumptuously prepare for himself a way to the immortal health of his body, which the dead...

Philosophers, his ancestors in this wicked world and true vale of miseries, of which they—as strangers and pilgrims of this world—never so much as dreamed.

<margin>
<insert>The Good vs. the Goodness of Plato</insert> 
God is to be seen beyond the horizon of eternity and on the other side of the wall of Paradise, which is the proper place of those who contemplate him. He who continues united with God in Christ, like God and the angels, never grows old. In vain will those followers <note>original: "husks & scraps"</note> of *Paracelsus* rise up, as they usually do, who exclaim that this interpretation is forced and far-fetched.
</margin>

**GOD** is the center of all creatures. The more any being draws near to him, the more blessed, less variable, and less changeable <note>original: "mutable"</note> they are. But the farther anything departs from that center or "The One"—that is, the immutable <note>unchangeable</note> will of God—toward the circumference, variety, and plurality of created things, the more unhappy, imperfect, and changeable it is. Blessedness is found in unity, not in the circumference; in Christ, not in the world, is peace and the rest of souls.

Therefore, whoever, through the immense goodness of God which goes before us without ceasing, shall forget all things and leave sensory and temporary things behind—which are to be used only during our passage through life—and shall be united to that one center, they grow young rather than old. This is the true "Long Life" of the <term>Cabalists</term> <note>mystics who followed the Jewish tradition of the Kabbalah</note> and of *Paracelsus*, which he so often and so earnestly begged for through prayer and holy hope in his hymns and soliloquies: the true Enochian <note>relating to the biblical figure Enoch, who was said to have walked with God and was taken to heaven without dying</note> life.

Conversely, anyone who is not joined to this most united, fountain-like, and singular Unity through devotion <note>original: "adhesion"</note> must of necessity perish forever. They will be separated from light and life by the "second death" and cast into the utter darkness of the <term>caliginous</term> <note>misty, dark, or gloomy</note> world; this deprivation and lack of the sight of God is the most bitter of all punishments.

**First,** because in **NATURAL THINGS** there is no clearly appointed date on which we shall die. I speak here philosophically of "natural death," which is only the loss of natural moisture and heat <note>original: "wasting of the Naturall Moysture and Heat." By that limit, we are forced not only to pay the debt of nature <note>A common historical idiom for dying.</note> but are also compelled to undergo the punishment for sin. 

Death is the boundary which we cannot pass; yet there is no specific day or hour set for us, for by the grace of **God**, we live out a span of time without strictly fixed hours. Just as **God** has numbered our hairs, so also does He count our years, leaving them within our power. And because it was the good pleasure of **God** that man should live forever, it is easy to discern that—for the lawful reproduction and growth of mankind—a long and lasting human life in this world is not displeasing to Him. This is especially true if that life is spent in the fear and service of one's Creator. 

However, this life always falls short of that ultimate and fixed limit, or the determined point of **DIVINE PREDESTINATION**, which is unknown to mortals. This boundary was imposed on our first parents and their heirs because of their fall from Original Righteousness <note>The theological state of innocence held by Adam and Eve before the Fall.</note>, and no man can go beyond it. ...glory of God and the benefit of my neighbors' welfare, let them not vex and trouble without cause the hardworking diligence of proven experience, and other men's toil and sweat, with their proud and rash criticisms like *Aesop's Crow* <note>original: "Æsop's Crow"; a reference to the fable where a crow decks itself in the feathers of other birds to appear more beautiful than it is</note>. Nor should they reproach them for a patched-together and mixed hodgepodge of good and bad (for to separate the grain from the chaff, and the true from the false, is sometimes a most tedious and difficult task; let those who have toiled and sweated in similar work be the judges).

Nor let them, like *Timon* the Man-hater <note>Timon of Athens, a legendary figure famous for his misanthropy or hatred of mankind</note>, observe a stubborn or churlish spirit and then pass it down to posterity, or publish to all the world their cruel and detestable inhumanity and most unrighteous hatred of the Truth by rising up against it. This is unless—being entirely excluded from the company of learned men instead of receiving an answer—they wish to be called stubborn enemies of mankind and adversaries of public safety, who (as previously mentioned) are justly to be dismissed <term>original: "casheer'd"; to be formally discarded or rejected from service or respect</term>.

And let them not frighten those who are students of the truth, who receive our labors and faithful diligence kindly and with appreciation. Or, if they must discourage anyone, let them open their own fountains of knowledge, having been given the opportunity here to publish their own *Observations*. Let them take their lighted candle from under the basket <note>A biblical reference to Matthew 5:15, meaning one should share their knowledge or light rather than hiding it</note>, lest the 
<margin>Matthew 21:19</margin> 
curse of the fig tree <note>In the Gospel of Matthew, Jesus curses a fig tree for bearing no fruit, symbolizing a failure to produce good works</note> befall them.

<meta>This page concludes the author's appeal to his peers, urging them to advance the field of medicine through chemistry and alchemical practice.</meta>

<margin>For who can find out the end of medicine?</margin>Inspired by my example, as becomes good and sincere citizens of the medical and alchemical <term>Spagyrick: Referring to alchemy, specifically the process of separating and recombining natural substances to create medicine.</term> profession, I hope others will bring forth better things than these from their own experience. For medicine has certainly not yet reached the limit of perfection; instead, many things remain to be discovered by future ages. We must help poor *Lazarus* <note>original: "Lazarus"; a biblical figure representing the poor, sick, and suffering</note>, not with logical <note>original: "Sylogistical"</note> or empty religious <note>original: "Levitical"</note> words, but with the help and aid of a Good Samaritan. 

If they do this, and cast away the signs of cursed laziness—acting like drones in their hollow cracks and outcries which at least make a terrible noise—they may instead become bees. Then, in grateful agreement, godly love, and mutual duty, they may join with us in a union of alchemical honey-making <note>original: "Spagyrick mellifice"</note>, separating themselves from the common crowd and abstaining from all corruption <note>original: "fornication"; here used metaphorically to mean the debasement or spiritual pollution of the medical art</note>. 

And they may *truly* and indeed maintain and defend the excellence of chemistry against all those who reproach it. With their skill and learning, without envy or malicious talk, they should endeavor to make this good effort of ours even better than the work itself. There is no doubt that, alongside other secret sciences which still lie in the dark, that ancient, true, and philosophical medicine may soon be restored. This medicine is not yet fully known because of the long passage and injury of time, as well as the unworthiness of our age (doubtless due to the gathered sins of men). However, by heavenly assistance, it may in a short time be restored to its lost luster and ancient splendor, to the great benefit of all mankind's health and the proper honor of alchemical physicians. May the immense sea of divine mercy be pleased to use their efforts and pains as an instrument and pen to accomplish such a healthy and saving work.

### TEXT I.

<margin>All creatures in the world have but one principle <note>In early modern philosophy, a "principle" refers to the fundamental source or primary substance from which everything originates.</note>.</margin>
**A**LL created things are of a fragile and mortal nature, and all had, at first, only one single principle or beginning. In this principle, all things under the canopy of heaven <note>original: "Cope of Heaven"</note> were enclosed and lay hidden. This is to be understood as meaning that all things proceeded out of one *Matter*, and not that every individual thing arose from its own separate matter. This common matter of all

<margin>What the Great Mystery is.</margin>
<margin>The Great Mystery is the mother of all things.</margin>
...things is the ~~Great Mystery~~, which no certain essence or prefigured or formed <term>Idea</term> <note>original: "Idæa"; referring to the archetypal forms or blueprints of things in the mind of the creator</note> could comprehend, nor could it comply with any property, being altogether void of color and elementary nature. The scope of this ~~Great Mystery~~ is as large as the sky <note>original: "Firmament"</note>. And this *Great Mystery was the mother of all the Elements, and the Grandmother of all the Stars, trees, and fleshly creatures.* As children are born of a mother, so all created things—whether they are perceivable by the senses or not <note>original: "sensible or insensible"</note>, and all things whatsoever—~~were uniformly brought out of the Great Mystery.~~ So it is that the Great Mystery is the only mother of all perishable things, out of which they all sprung. They did not come in an order of succession or continuation, but they all came forth together and at once, in one creation, substance, matter, form, essence, nature, and inclination.

---

## Text 2.

<margin>How man's urine is made.</margin>
That this *Mystery* was such as no other creature ever was, or ever saw the like, and yet was the *first matter* <note>original: "first matter"; in alchemy, the "prima materia" or the original formless base of all substance</note> out of which all mortal things proceeded, cannot be better understood than by the *urine of man*. Urine is made of *water, air, earth, and fire*, but is none of these, nor was it like any of them; yet all the Elements, through another generation, arise from there and so pass into a third generation. But since urine is only a created thing, there may be some 
<margin>The Great Mystery was not created.</margin>
difference between this and that. For the *Great Mystery is increased*, and was prepared by
->that<-

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the "Great Artificer" and the nature of the "Great Mystery" (Mysterium Magnum) as the origin of all creation.</meta>

<page-num>3</page-num>
<header>Written to the Athenians.</header>
<sig>A a 3</sig>

...that great Artificer <note>original: "Artificer"; referring to a divine creator or master craftsman of the universe</note>. There shall never be any other like it; nor does this return, or is it brought back again, into itself. *For just as cheese no longer becomes milk, so neither does that which is generated* [from the Mystery] *return into its original matter.* And though all things indeed may at length be reduced to their pristine nature and condition, yet they do not return again to the *Mystery* <note>original: "Mysterie"; in Paracelsian philosophy, the "Mysterium Magnum" is the primordial substance from which all things were created</note>. That which is once consumed can by no means be recovered again. But it may return into that which existed before the *Mystery*.

### TEXT 3.

<margin>How the great *Mystery* is the mother of all things.</margin>
Furthermore, although the *Great Mystery* is indeed the mother of all things, both *perceivable and unperceivable* <note>original: "sensible and insensible"; things that can be perceived by the senses and those that are spiritual or hidden</note>; yet not all things that grow, nor living creatures, nor similar things, were formed directly within it. Instead, we are to consider it in this way: that it left and assigned general mysteries to all things—that is, to men and living creatures—and to those of one kind it gave a mystery to reproduce <note>original: "propagate"</note> themselves after their own form. In the same way, it bestowed a unique mystery on every other thing to bring forth its own shape by itself. From the same origin also (which the *primary mystery* ordained), spring those mysteries from which another mystery may be produced. For a Star <note>original: "Star (alias mucke)"; "Star" refers to an astral or life-giving influence, while "muck" refers to the decaying organic matter from which insects were thought to spontaneously generate</note> is the mystery of beetles, flies, and gnats, etc. Milk is the mystery of cheese, butter, and such things. Cheese is the mystery of worms that breed in it. So again, worms are the mystery...

<meta>catchword: of</meta>

<margin>Mysteries are of two sorts.</margin>
...of perished cheese. <note>The author likely uses the analogy of "perished cheese" to describe the decay or corruption of material substances.</note> And thus, there are two sorts of mysteries: the **Great Mystery** <note>original: "Great Mysterie"; a term often referring to the *Mysterium Magnum*, the undifferentiated source of all creation in Hermetic philosophy</note>, which is uncreated, and the rest, being of the same nature, which are called **special mysteries**.

---

## TEXT 4.

<margin>All things came out of the Great Mystery at one and the same time.</margin>
Now, since it is plain that all perishable things sprang and arose out of the **uncreated mystery**, we must know that no created thing was brought forth sooner or later than another. Nothing was made separately; rather, everything was brought forth all together and at once. For the **Greatest Secret**—that is, the goodness of the Creator—created or placed all things together within that which is uncreated. This was not done in a finished form, essence, or through specific qualities; instead, everything lay hidden in the uncreated state just as an image or statue exists within a piece of timber. For just as the statue is not seen until the waste wood is cut away so that it may appear, so it is in the uncreated mystery: that which is physical and perceptible, and that which is imperceptible, both came forth and achieved their own form and kind through an ordained <term>separation</term> <note>original: "instituted separation"; the process by which distinct things emerge from a unified source</note>. Here, there was no hacking or hewing, but everything transitioned into form and essence.

<margin>The wonderful art of the Creator.</margin>
Never was there a workman found anywhere so accurate and industrious in the act of separation; with such skill, he could make even the smallest and lightest grain useful and put life into it.

<summary>The author explains that all created things emerged simultaneously from the "Great Mystery" (the uncreated source), comparing the process of creation to a statue being revealed from a block of wood.</summary>
<keywords>Great Mystery, uncreated mystery, creation, separation, essence, Creator, cosmology, Paracelsian philosophy</keywords>

### TEXT 5.

<margin>A comparison between the mystery and a medicine.</margin>
Understand it this way: it is not as if a house were built out of the *Great Mystery* <term>Great Mystery: Also known as the *Mysterium Magnum*, a term used by the author (likely Paracelsus) to describe the primordial substance or "mother" of all things from which the universe was created.</term>, or that living creatures were first gathered together and laid in a heap and then perfected—nor the other things that grow likewise. Instead, it is as a *Physician* makes a compound of many virtues <note>original: "vertues"; in this context, it refers to the medicinal powers or essential properties of a substance</note>, even though the material is only one thing, in which none of those virtues appear because they lie hidden within it. <margin>How all things were in the mystery.</margin> So we must think that all sorts of creatures under heaven were set in order and put into the *Great Mystery*. They were not there perfectly according to their substance, form, and essence, but after another subtle manner of perfection (unknown to mortal man) whereby all things were enclosed into one. We all were created from that which is frail and mortal, and we are born much in the manner of Saturn <note>In alchemy, Saturn represents the process of "lead" or the initial, dark state of matter that must be broken down or separated to reveal hidden forms.</note>, who in the separation of himself brings forth all forms and colors, yet none of them appear visible in him. Since the mysteries of Saturn yield such offspring, there is no doubt that the *Great Mystery* contains this miracle within it. In its separation, all other superfluous <note>original: "superfluous"; extra or unnecessary parts</note> things are cut off. <margin>What kind of separation Saturn has.</margin> Yet, there is nothing so needless or useless that it cannot bring forth from itself some other increase, or suitable matter for it.

### TEXT 6.

Know therefore that several and various chips or fragments fell out in the cutting or carving—

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the "great mystery" (Mysterium Magnum) and how the Creator separated the primary matter of the universe into distinct forms. The catchword from the previous page was "of," leading into "of the great mystery."</meta>

<page-num>6</page-num>
<header>The first Book of Philosophy,</header>

<margin>
A twofold art of the Creator in the separation of the Great Mystery.
</margin>
...of the *great mystery* <note>original: *great mysterie*; in Paracelsian philosophy, the *Mysterium Magnum* is the primordial, undifferentiated substance from which all specific things were created.</note>, of which some became flesh, occurring in infinite sorts and forms. Others were sea monsters, also of wonderful variety; some became herbs; others turned into wood; and an abundance became stones and metals. At least two ways or methods of Art may be considered by those who ask how the omnipotent God carved out these things. First, in that He always ordained life and growth <note>original: "increase"</note>. Secondly, in that it was not just a single type of matter that fell away everywhere in the same way. For if a statue is cut out of a piece of timber, all its chips are wood. But here it was not so; instead, every thing received its own form and motion independently.

### T E X T 7.

<margin>
The mystery contained all things but without difference or distinction.
</margin>
Thus the distribution followed the operation of the *great mystery*, and the things that were separated from what was superfluous <note>In this context, "superfluous" refers to the byproducts or "chips" left over during the creation of a specific form; the author argues that even these leftovers became other creatures or materials.</note> appeared more excellent. At the same instant, various other things also proceeded out of those very superfluous materials that were chipped off. For the *great mystery* was not composed of the elements <note>The four classical elements: Earth, Air, Fire, and Water.</note>, though the elements themselves lay hidden within it. Nor was it a fleshly thing <note>original: "carnall"</note>, though all sorts of men were contained within it. Neither was it wood or stone; rather, the matter was such that it contained every mortal thing in its essence without difference or distinction, and afterward endowed every one of them with its own particular essence and form during the separation. Inasmuch, then, as the *great mystery* <note>The *Mysterium Magnum*, a term often used in Hermetic philosophy to describe the primordial "womb" or source of all creation.</note> was full of such Essence and Deity, along with the presence of the most eternal One, the SEPARATION occurred before any creature existed. When this process began, every creature sprang forth and appeared in its majesty, power, and free will. In this state, it shall continue to flourish until the end of the world, or *that great harvest*, when all things shall be ripe with fruit, which shall then be gathered and carried into the barn. *For the harvest is the conclusion of the fruit’s growth, and it intends nothing but the physical <note>original: corporall</note> destruction of all things.* And though their number is almost infinite, there is only one single harvest, when the entire Creation shall be reaped and carried into the barn. 

Nor will this harvest—the end of all things—be any less admirable than that *great mystery* was wonderful in the beginning, even though the free power of things is the cause of their mutual affection and destruction. For there is nothing that does not possess both love and hatred. Free will flourishes and is expressed through virtues, but acts as either a friend or a foe in our works. ### TEXT 11.

<margin>The first Elements arose from the separation of the mystery.</margin>

<margin>What the air is.</margin>
When the *great mystery* <note>original: "great mysterie"; in Paracelsian philosophy, this refers to the primal, unformed state of all matter before creation.</note> first separated all things, the *first separation was of the Elements*. Thus, before all other things, the Elements burst forth into their active state and essence. The fire became heaven and the wall of the firmament <note>The sphere of the stars or the boundary of the physical universe.</note>. The air was made into a void space where nothing appears or can be seen; it occupies that place where there is no substance or physical matter. This is the chest or enclosure of the invisible Destinies <note>Likely referring to spiritual or celestial forces that govern fate.</note>.

<margin>What the water is.</margin>
The water turned into liquid and took its place within the channels and hollows of the center, contained within the other Elements and the sky. This is the dwelling place of the nymphs and monsters of the sea.

<margin>What the Earth is. It is supported by the pillars of the Archaltes.</margin>
The earth solidified into dry land. It is not held up by any of the other Elements but is propped up by the pillars of the <term>Archaltes</term> <note>A term used by Paracelsus to describe the invisible spiritual foundations or "vaults" that support the physical earth.</note>. These are the strange and wonderful works of God. The earth is the storehouse of all things that grow, which are nourished by it. This kind of separation was the beginning of all created things and the first distribution of both these elements and all other creatures.

<margin>How the stars emerged from heaven.</margin>
stars emerged from the heavens through separation alone, just as "flowers of silver" <note>This likely refers to an alchemical process where silver crystallizes into tree-like or flower-like shapes, often called the "Tree of Diana."</note> rise and separate themselves. ## TEXT 14

<margin>The separation of water and its various forms.</margin>
Through the separation of the elements, the water was gathered into the place which the <term>mystery</term> <note>original: "mysterie"; in Paracelsian philosophy, the *Mysterium Magnum* is the primordial substance or divine plan from which all things are differentiated</note> had allotted to it. Thus, everything that lay hidden within its elementary power and property was more fully divided by a second separation. The water was parted into many special mysteries, all of which had their <term>matrix</term> <note>the womb, source, or environment from which a specific thing originates</note> from the element of water. One part of it became fish, which are of many forms and kinds—some like beasts, some salty. Much of it became sea plants, such as <margin>Coral, trine, and citrons: sea plants.</margin> **corals**, **trines**, and **citrons** <note>archaic terms for specific types of sea growths or corals thought to resemble land-based fruit or plants</note>. A great deal of it became sea monsters, which exist contrary to the usual and natural course of the elements. Very much became <margin>Nymphs, Sirens, Drames, Lorind, Nesder.</margin> nymphs, sirens, drames, lorind, and nesder <note>these names refer to various classes of water spirits or "elementals" categorized in Paracelsus's unique system of natural philosophy</note>. Some of these are <term>rational creatures</term> <note>original: "reasonable creatures"; beings possessing intelligence or a soul</note>, having something eternal within their bodies and the ability to reproduce. Some, however, die completely, and some are separated again over time.

For the perfect separation of the element of water is not yet finished. But as the "great harvest" <note>likely a reference to the end of a cosmic cycle or the final refinement of nature</note> hastens and approaches, a new increase may spring up every year within the element of water. This separation occurs at the same instant the other elements are separated—in a single day's work and by the motion of <term>sequestration</term> <note>original: "sequestration"; the act of setting apart or isolating different substances from a mixture</note>. Thus, every living thing in the water was created and made manifest at once, in a single minute and moment of time, through this separation.

### T E X T 15.

<margin>The separation of the earth and its kinds.</margin>
In the same way, when the element of earth was separated from the rest, the earthy separation occurred—namely, *the separation of all things that grow, or did grow, in or out of the earth*. For at the first creation, the four elements lay hidden within everything alike in the *great mystery* <note>original: "great mysterie"; the *Mysterium Magnum*, a Paracelsian term for the primordial matter or "mother" of all things</note>. These things were also separated in the same way and in a single instant, and were divided among themselves one after another by a second separation, which is the elementary one. And through this kind of elementary separation from the element of earth, *sentient and insentient things, those that are eternal and those that are not eternal*, were parted from one another, with each obtaining its own unique essence and free power. All that was of a woody nature was made into wood.

<margin>The diversity of minerals.</margin>
The next was the ores <note>original: "mines"</note> of metals. A third became *marcasite* <term>iron pyrite or similar crystalline minerals</term>, *talc* <note>original: "talke"</note>, *bismuth* <note>original: "bismute"</note>, *garnet* <note>original: "pomegranate"; historically, garnets were often called "pomegranate stones" due to their color and seed-like shape</note>, *metallic cobalt*, *millstone* <note>original: "milsto"</note>, and many other things. A fourth became precious stones of many sorts and shapes, as well as stones, sand, and lime. A fifth was made into fruit, flowers, herbs, and seeds. A sixth was made into *sentient living creatures*, of which some share in eternity (such as *humans*), while others do *not* (such as *calves, sheep, etc.*). Many kinds and differences among these could be listed; for many more varieties were separated within the earthy element than in any other.

### TEXT 16.

<margin>How the complexions <term>The "complexion" refers to the balance of the four primary qualities (heat, cold, moisture, and dryness) that define a substance's nature.</term> were brought forth. <insert>contrast</insert></margin>
After the four elements were separated from each other from the beginning out of the single <note>original: onely</note> prime matter (as has been said)—in which matter their qualities and essences did not yet exist—*the complexions and natures emerged through that separation.* The *hot* and *dry* qualities went into the heavens and the firmament <note>The "firmament" refers to the sphere of the stars and planets</note>, each splitting into its own specific property. The *hot* and *moist* qualities went into the air, through which the heat and moisture are invisibly separated. The *cold* and *moist* turned into the sea and the adjoining regions. The *cold* and *dry* descended <note>original: degenerated</note> into the earth and all earthly things. And even contrary substances arose from the separation of the elements—substances that have no resemblance at all to the elements from which they came. 
<margin>Lime comes out of the fire, yet is not of a fiery nature.</margin>
Of this sort is *lime* <note>Often referring to quicklime, produced by heating limestone</note>, which by its own nature is not fire, even though it arises out of the fire. The reason for this is that, during the separation of nature, the breakdown <note>original: dissolution</note> of the substance moved too far away from the fiery nature.

<margin>Four types of fire.</margin>
<margin>Various degrees of fire.</margin>
...the element; for fire has both cold and moisture in it. There are four types of fire. Therefore, the colors produced by fire are not always the same. One fire causes a white and azure <note>original: "azure," a bright blue</note> color. Dry fire makes a red and green color. One type of moist fire makes an ashy and black color, while another moist fire produces colors like saffron <note>a deep yellow-orange</note> and red. For this reason, one form of generation <note>original: "procreation," here meaning the production or creation of substances</note> is hotter than another, because one fire is more or less intense in its degree than another. There is not just one simple and single fire; rather, there are hundreds of types of fire, yet not a single one is of the same degree as another. The generation of each of them, therefore, arises from its own source <note>original: "subject"</note>, ordained as a kind of mystery.

## TEXT 17.

<margin>Various natures of water.</margin>
<margin>The differences between waters.</margin>
Nor did water possess only one kind of nature <note>original: "complexion," referring to the temperament or internal balance of a substance</note>. For there were infinite types of water within that element, which yet were all truly waters. A philosopher cannot assume that the element of water is only cold and moist by its nature. It can be a hundred times colder without being more moist, and yet its nature must be judged by its heat as much as its coldness. The element of water does not exist or flourish only in one degree of cold and moisture; no, it is not entirely of a single degree. Some waters are springs <note>original: "fountaines"</note>, which are of many sorts. Some are seas, which are also many and diverse. ### TEXT 18.

This fire should not be considered the element itself; it is only the consumption or wasting away of the earth's substance.

<margin>How a philosopher should name things.</margin>
...it watery fire. Again, whereas the fire of earth <note>original: "fire of earth"; likely referring to physical, terrestrial fire like a campfire or candle flame</note> will burn and blaze, it is not therefore to be accounted as the element of fire, even though it is somewhat like fire. He is but a foolish and sensory-based <note>original: "sensuall"; here meaning one who relies only on physical senses rather than intellect</note> philosopher who names the element according to what he perceives. Instead, he should think that the element itself is something far different than such a fire as this. And for what cause? Everything that moistens is not the element of water.

<margin>Earth may be reduced into water.</margin>
Even the element of earth may be turned into water, yet it remains earth still. Likewise, whatever is in the earth is of the element of earth. For a thing is, and is known by, the property of that from which it proceeded and to which it is similar.

<margin>A flint and chalcedony produce fire.</margin>
A man may strike fire out of a flint and chalcedony <note>a type of silica mineral or quartz</note>. That is not elemental fire, but a strong expression resulting from great hardness.

### T E X T 19.

The element of air has many offspring within it, all of which are still merely air. Every philosopher should well understand this: that no element can beget another thing out of itself except that which it is in itself.

<margin>Like produces like.</margin>
Like always begets its like. So then, seeing that the air is invisible, it can bring nothing visible out of itself. And because it is impalpable <note>cannot be felt or touched</note>, it can produce nothing that may be touched. Therefore (if I may say so) it does melosinate <note>original: "meloſinate"; an archaic alchemical term likely referring to the process of an element bearing fruit or forming a specific substance within another medium</note>.

<margin>What the nymphs are.</margin>
In the same way, a connection can be made between the air and a human, as happens through spirits in all witchcraft and enchantments. The same may be said here as was said of the nymphs; although they live in the element of water and are composed of nothing but water, they still have the freedom to interact with things on the Earth and to procreate with them. A similar materialization <term>original: compaction; the process of becoming solid or physical</term> also occurs from the air, which can be seen and felt. However, this is not a procreation from the first separation <term>separation: a technical term in alchemy and early science referring to the division of the four elements from the "prime matter" or "mysterium magnum"</term>, but only a secondary result. For just as a beetle is born from dung, so a monster of the airy element may assume a bodily shape with airy words, thoughts, and deeds through a mixture with that which is earthy. Nevertheless, these kinds of miracles and consequences eventually dissolve <note>original: decay</note> back into the air, just as nymphs turn back into water—exactly as a man is consumed by rot and turned to earth because he originated from there.

Thunder is, as it were, the harvest of the stars at the very moment it was ready to act according to its nature. ## T E X T 21.

<margin>What the fourth separation is.</margin>
As we have seen a threefold separation made out of the mystery <note>The "Mysterium Magnum," or the primordial source of all matter.</note> into three sorts of forms, it remains now that we consider the fourth and last separation of all, after which there shall be no more; for then all the others shall perish and be no longer a mystery. After the fourth, all things shall be reduced into their first principle, and that only remain which was before the *great mystery*, and is eternal. Which is not to be understood as if I could be turned into anything, or as though anything could be made of me after the last separation, unless by death: for I shall be brought to nothing, just as in respect of my beginning I came out of nothing.

## TEXT 23.

<margin>What mortal things are eternal.</margin>
<margin>Man is a companion of the eternal.</margin>
The final separation is the dissolution of all creatures, where one thing consumes and perishes after another; through this, the lifespan of all those things is known. When creatures first existed, they did not contain total ruin within themselves, because a new seed <term>seed: the reproductive or regenerative principle in nature</term> always takes the place of the old, decayed thing. Thus, there is something eternal and not subject to ruin within mortal things, achieved through the renewal of another seed—a concept the Philosopher <note>original: "the Philosopher"; likely referring to Aristotle or the established academic authorities of the time, whom the author frequently critiques</note> does not understand. No seed by itself admits or creates what is eternal. Yet it does undergo putrefaction <term>putrefaction: the process of decay or breaking down, often seen in alchemy as a necessary step for rebirth</term> when the eternal part is taken into the eternal realm. 

In this regard, man alone among all other creatures has that which is eternal within himself, joined with that which is mortal. As previously stated, the mortal and eternal are joined together. Understand that the mortal part prepares an essence in the stomach <note>In the chemical philosophy of this period, the stomach was viewed as an internal alchemist or "Archeus" that separated life-sustaining essences from waste</note> and sustains the physical needs of the body. The only reason for this is so that the eternal part of man might live forever, while the mortal part might die according to its own frailty. As the body is, so is the eternal part that comes from that body. This is what confuses all philosophy: that the mortal part should dominate and, as it were, hold sway as it pleases over that which is eternal, and that the eternal should also depend on man—who

# The First Book of Philosophy

...is thereby made more a companion of that which is eternal, than if both his mortal and eternal parts flowed from himself. From this we may conclude that all creatures should live together—the rational <note>original: "reasonable"</note> and the irrational—one being of service to another, the eternal planted into the mortal, and these two dwelling together. 

<margin>Destruction comes from opposing forces <note>original: "contraries"</note>.</margin>

Philosophy teaches that those things that live together *without squabbling and fighting, without guile and deceit, without good and evil,* cannot be destroyed or consumed. This destruction would otherwise occur if one thing were to oppose another. Those who do not have the eternal dwelling within them have no knowledge or judgment. But those things in which the eternal exists cannot be destitute of understanding. When things fell into such disagreement as to clash with one another, one eternal being was forced to give an account and make satisfaction for wrongs to another. 

<margin>What things are subject to destruction.</margin>

And because recompense belongs to the eternal, it must not be repaid by that which is mortal. Although bodies may pacify and tolerate one another, if anything is left here, it is that which is eternal. Therefore, only that which is eternal within us is judged. And though one may make demands of <note>original: "exact upon"</note> or judge another, all mortal things that contain the eternal must die whether they wish to or not, so that only the eternal shall remain behind, without the company of the body. Thus, the judgment is finished. For only that is eternal, nor is there any more of it to come in the final destruction of every mortal thing. Now, if those things that had the eternal within them have perished in this way,

These things belong to the eternal. Since it must be so, we all must of necessity be brought and come together. Thus have we found the dissolution <note>The breaking down or ending</note> of all things.
