## THE
# Second Book,
### Text I.

<margin>The difference between the creating Gods.</margin>
**S**eeing, then, there was something by which, when it was separated, all things were created: first we must conclude that there is some difference among the Gods, which is this. Since the things created are divided into *eternal* and *mortal*, the reason for this is because there was another creator of the mysteries <note>original: "mysteries"; likely referring to the hidden or spiritual elements of the cosmos</note>, besides the chiefest and most high. For the most high Creator ought to be the judge and corrector of all creatures; He would know how much was bestowed on them whereby they might do either good or evil, even though they did not receive those impulses directly from Him. Moreover, the creatures are always <margin>Man's inclination is toward evil.</margin> urged and provoked toward evil, compelled to it by the fates, the stars, and by the infernal one <note>The devil or a hellish power</note>. This could by no means have been the case if they had proceeded from the Most High Himself—that we should be forced into those properties of good and evil—but we should in all things have had free will, without any such violent instigation.

<meta>catchword: yet</meta>

<summary>This page argues for a hierarchy of divine beings, suggesting that while a "Most High" God acts as a judge, a secondary creator is responsible for the "mysteries" of the world and the influences of fate and evil that challenge human free will.</summary>

### TEXT 2.

<margin>The four elements are the mothers of all things.</margin>
Now, if it were necessary that all things that were made should consist of and proceed from only four things—as we know they did through the process of separation—then those four alone must be the <term>matrices</term> <note>original: "matrixes," meaning the wombs or source-points from which something originates</note> of all creatures, which we call the elements. And though every creature is still an element or may have some share of an element, it is not like the physical element itself, but like the Spirit of the Element. Nothing can exist without an element. Nor can the elements themselves stand together. There is nothing that consists of four, three, or even two elements; rather, one element stands by itself separately, and every creature has but one element. They are altogether blind who take that which is *moist* for the element of water, or that which *burns* for the element of fire. We must not limit...

<meta>This page continues the philosophical discourse "Written to the Athenians," attributed to Paracelsus, concerning the nature of the elements and the "first matter" of the universe.</meta>

<margin>What an Element is.</margin>
An element is to a body, substance, or quality what the soul is to the human form. That which we see is only the subject or receptacle <note>The physical "receptacle" or container of the essence.</note>. The element is a spirit; it lives and grows in those things, just as the soul lives in the body. This is the "first matter" of the elements, which can be neither seen nor felt, and yet is present in all things.

<margin>What the first matter of an Element is.</margin>
The first matter of the elements is nothing other than the life force that creatures possess. If any creature dies, it no longer exists within any element, but returns to the "ultimate matter," <note>In Paracelsian philosophy, the "ultimate matter" is the final state of a substance after its life or "virtue" has been spent.</note> which has no taste, force, or virtue.

## Text 3.

<margin>All things consist of the four Elements.</margin>
Given that all things that could be created were made of four "mothers"—namely, the four elements—take further notice that those four elements were sufficient for all things that were to be created. It was not necessary for there to be more or fewer. In mortal things, no more than four natures can exist together. However, in immortal things, "temperaments" <note>Refers to a balanced state of qualities (temperamentum) that transcends the physical decay of individual elements.</note> may exist, even though the elements themselves cannot.

<margin>How the Element differs from the temperament.</margin>
Whatever is (as I call it) an <term>elementure</term> <note>original: "elementure"; a physical entity or object composed of and governed by the elements.</note> can be dissolved. But on the contrary, a "temperature" <note>A stable, balanced state of being.</note> cannot be dissolved. For such is its condition that nothing can be added to it or taken from it; no part of it can putrefy or perish. And seeing that the physical condition is mortal, as has been said, we must know that all things exist within four natures, and that every nature retains the name of its corresponding element.

<margin>The names of the four Elements.</margin>
As the element of fire is hot, the element of earth is cold, and the element of water is...

<summary>This page defines the "element" as a spiritual essence or "first matter" that animates physical bodies, distinguishing between mortal "elementures" which can decay and immortal "temperaments."</summary>

--- Page 81 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the singular nature of the four elements—fire, earth, water, and air—and their relationship to human health and anatomy.</meta>

<page-num>30</page-num>
The Second Book of Philosophy

<margin>The nature of the Elements is simple.</margin>
...is moist, and the element of air is dry. Here we must also consider that each of these natures is uniquely its own, existing separately. For fire is only hot, and not dry or moist. The earth is only cold, not dry or moist. The water is only moist, not hot or cold. The air is only dry, not hot or cold. Therefore, they are called "elements" because they have only one simple nature, rather than a double one. However, their manifestation through all creatures must be understood as an element that can exist within a physical substance and body, where it can perform its work. The highest knowledge concerning the elements is this: that every one of them has only one simple nature—either moist, dry, cold, or hot.

<margin>The elements and simple spirits.</margin>
This is due to the condition of spirits. For every spirit has a simple, not a double, nature; and the elements are the same.

# TEXT 4.

<margin>Where colic comes from.</margin>
Though we mortals have compounds within us—such as heat and moisture—it is quite different from what the ancients <note>Likely referring to the followers of Galen and the traditional theory of the four humors.</note> imagined. For <term>colic</term> <note>original: "Colick"; a term for severe, fluctuating abdominal pain</note> comes from the element of fire, yet it is not a compound of heat and dryness; it is only hot. The same applies to other <term>complexions</term> <note>original: "complexions"; in pre-modern medicine, this referred to the specific balance of bodily qualities like heat, cold, moisture, or dryness.</note>. Therefore, if we find any disease mixed with both heat and dryness, we may suppose that two elements are present: one in the liver and another in the spleen, and so on in the other <term>members</term> <note>original: "members"; here meaning internal organs.</note>. There are not two elements in a single organ. For it is certain that every organ has its own specific element, a matter which we leave to the <term>physicians</term>.

<summary>The author argues that the four elements are "simple" because each possesses only one quality, and asserts that medical conditions involving multiple qualities are actually the result of different elements affecting different organs simultaneously.</summary>
<keywords>elements, simple nature, fire, air, water, earth, colic, complexions, anatomy, medicine, physicians</keywords>

<margin>The elements are not mixed.</margin>
...to define. But it cannot well be maintained that two elements should exist together, or that one and the same element should be both hot and moist. Nor can there be any such compound. There are no compounded <term>elements</term> <note>In Aristotelian and Paracelsian physics, elements were the simplest building blocks of matter; the author argues they must remain pure.</note>, for the reason previously given. Where there is heat, there is neither cold, nor dryness <note>original: "drought"</note>, nor moisture. So where there is coldness, there is none of the others. The same may be said of moisture and dryness. Every <term>element</term> is simple and solitary by itself, not mixed in a composition. The possibility that <term>philosophers</term> discuss concerning a conjunction of the <term>elements</term> amounts to nothing. For no <term>element</term> of water has any heat in it. Nor can there be any heat in moisture. Every <term>element</term> is alone by itself. So also, cold cannot by itself endure dryness; it exists pure by itself. And let this much be said so that the proper essence of the <term>elements</term> may be understood. All dryness is a dissolution of cold. Just as moisture and dryness cannot be mixed, so much less can coldness and dryness or moisture—or heat and dryness—join or exist together. For just as heat and cold are opposites, so heat and cold are also opposed to moisture and dryness.

## Text 6.

<margin>A comparison between fire and the soul.</margin>
<margin>What fire is.</margin>
<margin>What the properties of all the elements are.</margin>
In order that we may more fully understand what an element is, we must know that an element is nothing but a soul. This is not to say that it is of the same essence as a soul, but rather that it has something similar to it. The difference between the soul of an element and the eternal soul is this: the soul of the elements is the life of all creatures. The fire that burns is not the element of fire as we see it; rather, its soul—which we cannot see—is the element and life of fire. Now, the element of fire may be no less present in a green stick than it is in an active fire, but the actual life of it is not the same there as it is in the fire.

This, then, is the difference between the soul and the life. If fire "lives," it burns; but if it is in the soul—that is, in its element—then it cannot burn. Nor does it follow that a cold thing must necessarily come from a cold element, for often it comes from a hot one. Indeed, many cold things come from the element of fire. Whatever grows does so because of the element of fire, though in another form. Whatever is fixed <term>fixt: stable, solid, or unchanging</term> is from the element of earth. That which nourishes is from the element of air. And that which consumes is from the element of water. To grow is the property only of fire. When that fails or goes out, there is no increase. Were it not for the element of earth, there would be no end to growth. It is that which

### T E X T 9.

<margin>The various offspring of the elements.</margin>
It is most obvious that from a single seed, the root sprouts into many shoots, then into the stalk; afterward, the branches shoot out, and finally the flower, fruit, and seed appear. It is exactly the same with the various offspring created from the four elements. All these creations that come from one element cling closely to each other, just as an <term>herb</term> <note>original: "hearb"</note> grows from one seed, though they are not all exactly identical to the seed they came from. The creatures which are made of the water are partly men <note>This refers to elemental beings such as Nymphs or Undines, which were believed to inhabit the water and resemble humans.</note>, partly living creatures, and partly the food for both. One element clearly reveals its own <term>signature</term> <note>The "Doctrine of Signatures" was the belief that the physical appearance of things in nature indicated their medicinal or spiritual purpose.</note>, its needs, and its <term>sustenance</term> <note>original: "sustentation"; meaning the means of support or nourishment.</note>; it also hints at its path and arrival. This can easily be known by the stars—not as though the stars guide and govern us, but because they keep pace with us and mirror the inward motion of our body.

<margin>A mistake about celestial minerals.</margin>
...have their minerals too, just as we do. The celestial firmament <note>The heavens or the sky</note> has minerals consisting of both flowers and stones, which we may rank among the miracles. However, we may easily be deceived here and quickly run ourselves aground while we insist <note>original: "stickle"</note> so much on reckoning natural processes as prodigies <note>original: "prodies"; marvelous or ominous events often seen as signs from God</note>, or believing that this or that color of the firmament foreshadows some unique event. Thus, we prophesy like prophets, whereas we should instead conclude that such things happen according to the natural course of the firmament. But if any such thing should happen at any time, we should believe that such was simply our natural course and condition. Meanwhile, if any part of the elements is faulty, that same fault will weaken the rest. For all things should run in a perfect and uninterrupted course. And although the other three elements serve to nourish us, they are also ready to serve the firmament, the air, and the water, as well as the things within them. One thing is nourished by another, like many trees in an orchard. We may notice the errors and failings of the firmament, just as the firmament observes our own defects. The same may be said of all the rest.

## TEXT 12.

<margin>How the Great Mystery may be known.</margin>
<margin>There are only four elements in all things.</margin>
There should be neither more nor less than four mothers <note>original: "mothers," here referring to the four elements (earth, air, fire, water) as the source of all things</note> of all things, as all procreations <note>original: "procreations," meaning the things created or produced from the elements</note> show. This is not to say that the Great Mystery <note>original: "great Mysterie," likely referring to the *Mysterium Magnum*, a concept in Paracelsian or Boehmean philosophy describing the primordial source of all existence</note> we are discussing can be discovered through a universal demonstration of what kind of thing it was according to its properties in the beginning. Rather, the Great Mystery is known and understood through the "last mysteries" and through the creations that sprang and proceeded from the first. It is not the beginning, but the end that makes a man a Master and a philosopher. The knowledge of a thing according to its perfect nature is found only at the end of its existence. 

Possibly more elements could have been made than there are now, but in the ultimate knowledge of all things, only four are found. And although we may suppose that it would have been easy for God—who created only four—to have made many more, yet when we see that all mortal things consist of only four, we may conclude that more than these could not well exist together. It is most likely that when these four elements perish, others shall arise according to every essence, unlike the former; or, that after the destruction of the creatures already made, there shall be a new Great Mystery, the knowledge of which will be greater and better than the first. But we do not present this here as a fundamental principle; yet he who would understand the beginning of the world must—

<summary>This text argues that the "Great Mystery" of creation is best understood by observing its final results rather than its origins, asserting that the four elements are the essential foundation of all mortal things.</summary>

### Text 13.

<margin>All the elements are not present in all things.</margin>
But although all things exist within these four elements, we do not mean that all four elements are present in every thing, or that all four elements reside in everything. The reason is that the world which is separated and created from the element of fire has no need for air, water, or earth. Likewise, the world of air needs none of the other three. This is also true for the worlds of earth and water. Regarding the elements, we do not teach that the world cannot be preserved without all four elements; rather, we teach that every thing is preserved by that specific single element from which it originated. 

And while I do not deny that the firmament <note>original: "firmament"; refers to the heavens or the sky, often conceptualized in this period as a physical structure or sphere.</note> nourishes the world through its elementary powers <margin>How the firmament nourishes the earth.</margin> which descend entirely as fire upon the earth, yet that nourishment is not strictly necessary. Nor will the world perish on its own, for it has enough to sustain itself, just as the other world maintains itself without the help of the earth. For example, the "earth" within water <note>The author is likely referring to the solid or sedimentary qualities found in water.</note> contributes nothing to water's true essence, nor does the "water" within the earth contribute to the earth's essence. The same is true for the air. But it is not enough for each world to exist alone or by itself within its element; rather, <margin>What the light of heaven is.</margin> the light from heaven acts as a kind of extract of the

<margin>Tronus, Turas, Samies.</margin>
...the four Elements, most excellent in their full and perfect properties <note>original: "propriety"</note>. But let no one think that the Sun or Planets received their light <note>original: "lustre"</note> or motion from the element of fire, but rather from the Mystery <term>In Paracelsian philosophy, the "Mysterium" is the primordial, divine source or womb from which all specific things are created.</term>. The brightness of the Firmament that irradiates the world did not flow from the element of fire, but from the mystery. The earth produces *Tronus*, the water *Ture* <note>The margin lists "Turas," likely the spiritual essence of water.</note>, and the air *Samies*: these proceed not from the Element itself, but from the Mystery, yet they reside within the Element. Thus, the four worlds that emerged from the Mysteries agree to help each other, nourishing and sustaining one another—not because of the nature of the elements themselves, for they are merely the physical elements.

### T E X T 14.

<margin>Man's life, sight, etc.—where it comes from.</margin>
It is not from the Elements that man lives, sees, hears, and so on, but from the mysteries, or rather from the "monarchy" <term>The "monarchy" refers to the overarching divine order or governing power of nature.</term>; and the same is true for all other things. The physical element is merely an inn for lodging and a meal for sustenance. Know also that whatever is eternal comes from the Mystery and is of the same substance. Dogs die, but their mystery does not. Man dies, but his mystery survives—and even more so his soul, by which he is many degrees more excellent than a dog. The same can be said of all things that grow. <margin>The mystery of all things shall at last be revealed.</margin> Therefore, there is a common mistake in thinking that creatures will not appear again; all creatures that ever existed shall appear, not in their physical essence as they do now, but mystically in the final, great "new mystery." <margin>What a mystery is</margin> We do not say that the mystery is an essence exactly like that which is immortal, but rather that it is perfectly a mystery.

<summary>This page argues that the life, light, and motion of all things—including the stars and humanity—originate from a divine "Mystery" rather than the physical elements. It suggests that while physical forms perish, their "mystery" remains eternal and will be revealed in a final mystical state.</summary>

# Written to the Athenians.
<page-num>43</page-num>

<margin>How the mystery and the Elements differ.</margin>
The Element of fire has a mystery <note>original: "mysterie"; in the context of Paracelsian philosophy, a *mysterium* refers to the hidden, essential, or spiritual core of a substance that gives it its properties</note> in it, from which the other three have their light, luster, influence, and growth—and not from the Element itself. Those mysteries also may exist without an Element, just as an Element may exist without a mystery.

<margin>What the Elements are, and what kind of mysteries they have.</margin>
Observe further, that the Element of air has a mystery in it, by which all the other three, and itself too, are nourished; not elementally of itself, but mystically by the Element.

The Element of Earth has in it a mystery of mansion <term>mansion: a stable dwelling place or fixed state of being</term> and fixation <term>fixation: an alchemical term for the process by which a volatile substance is made solid or "fixed" so that it no longer evaporates when heated</term>, by virtue of which the others continue and increase, so that nothing perishes.

The Element of water has a mystery of sustenance <note>original: "sustentation"</note> for all the rest, and preserves all that is in them from destruction. In this respect, there is a difference between an Element and a mystery: one is mortal and corruptible because it comes from the Elements; the other is durable in the Last Great Mystery, wherein all things shall be renewed, but nothing shall be made that did not exist before.

### T E X T 15.

<margin>The Elements are each alone.</margin>
We conclude then that all the Elements cannot be joined together; but rather, they exist solitarily and unmixed, being entirely airy, or fiery, or earthy, or watery. 

<margin>The elements nourish themselves.</margin>
We have also settled <note>original: "dispatch'd"</note> this point: that every Element maintains itself, and everything that comes from it, as its own world. Therefore, a medicine made of the Element of water will do no good to those things that belong to the Element of earth, or of any other Element, <margin>Nymphs, etc.</margin> but only to the Nymphs and Sirens <note>Paracelsus taught that each element was inhabited by specific elemental spirits; Nymphs and Sirens (often called Undines) were the spirits belonging to the water</note>,

<summary>This text explains that each of the four elements contains a "mystery" or essential spirit that provides specific properties like light, nourishment, stability, or sustenance. The author argues that elements remain distinct and unmixed, each supporting only the life and "medicines" relevant to its own domain and elemental spirits.</summary>

If then those three foreign worlds plant men in our world, as we have said, they are to be known in their whole essence as Gods in respect to us, because of that huge distance and the very strange essence which they possess.

<margin>How Elements may be joined.</margin>
But on the other hand, if any of us are caught away by them, there is a contrary abduction <note>original: "rape"</note> from us to them. Thus, one Element has no need of another: one is but the cabinet or receptacle of the other. Just as water and earth separate from each other, so air and fire have their own specific lots, without any other contact <note>original: "contiguity"</note>, but standing like walls, and according to the inclination of the mysteries out of all the four.

## The Second Book of Philosophy

<margin>
Mortal things leave behind them that which is immortal.

...without an element, and without a present essence; but the things that shall then remain will be more temperate and incorruptible. This must not be understood as referring to the spirit, but to nature, with this evidence: that something eternal takes the place of that which is mortal. For if an insensible plant perishes, its place is supplied by that which is eternal. Nor is there any one frail or mortal thing in all the world which does not substitute something eternal in its place. Nothing is empty or vain; no corruptible thing was created without a succession of that which is eternal. When all creatures come to an end, then those things that are eternal shall meet and come together—not only as nourishment, but rather to fulfill the office or chief rule of nature in both the mortal and eternal realms. Thus, the eternal is a sign or token of the breaking down <note>original: dissolution</note> of nature, and not the beginning or principle of created things; it is in all things, and no nature is destitute of it. And though the beings of fate <note>original: Fatalls</note>, such as the Melusines <note>a legendary female spirit of fresh water in sacred springs and rivers</note> and nymphs, shall also leave the eternal behind them, we shall say nothing of their corruptions at this time.

<margin>
There is a fourfold decay <note>original: putrefaction</note> of the creatures.
</margin>

As there are four worlds, so we must know that there is a varied decay <note>original: manifold putrefaction</note>. There is an earthy, an airy, a fiery, and a watery decay. Everything—including what was created with it, together with the eternal part that remains—is brought and turned to destruction. Yet those four forms of decay shall bring back their eternal essence into one likeness <note>original: similitude</note> with renown and glory, not through its works, but through its essence.

Yet it is possible for a person to know the *Great Turban*, even to its final analysis <note>original: "resolution"</note>. # The Second Book of Philosophy
<page-num>52</page-num>

The Evester <note>original: "Evester"; in Paracelsian philosophy, an Evester is a prophetic spirit or an archetypal double that exists for every object and person, reflecting its fate and nature.</note> in the world, by which all evil is known, and all whatsoever violates and breaks the law of nature. For we shall not know ourselves except by our own Evester. Every thing has an Evester, all of which likewise are prophets, whether they are rational or irrational, sentient or insentient.

<margin>What an Evester is.</margin>
The Evester is a spirit which teaches astronomy. This does not mean it is learned through birth charts <note>original: "nativities"</note> and prognostications from the stars; rather, its very essence <note>original: "esse"</note> (as I may say) is from the Evesters. Its being <note>original: "Ens"</note> is in these, like an image in a mirror <note>original: "glass"</note>, or like a shadow in water or on the earth. Just as growing things are increased and diminished, so it is with the stars. It is not that their course is such by their own nature, or that moisture and cold rise out of the earth, but only because the essence of the earth is such. It is shadowed in heaven only in parts, yet as an Evester, and not as a power.

# THE
## Third Book.

### Text I.

<margin>Nothing exists without a body.</margin>
**E**very thing that has a being must necessarily have a body. The manner and reason for this is so that we may understand that it is like a smoky spirit that has neither substance, nor body, nor can it be felt. And though it is neither of these, yet both bodies and substances may proceed out of it. We may understand this by considering fuming <term>arsenic</term> <note>original: "Arfnicke." In early modern alchemy, arsenic was known for its volatility and the thick white fumes it produced when heated, often used as a metaphor for the transition between spirit and matter.</note>; after the generation of a body, no more of that spirit is seen, just as if everything had been turned into a solid body. Yet this is not actually the case, for it still remains in a most subtle form in that place of generation. Thus, both the visible and the invisible are brought forth together through separation.

<margin>How all things are embodied.</margin>
In this way and manner, all things are propagated. Wood still has a surviving spirit from which it is separated. So do stones, and all other things, without exception. For their <term>essence</term> <note>the intrinsic nature or indispensable quality of a substance</note> still remains just as it was when it was separated from them.

<margin>What man is, and how he is made.</margin>
Man likewise is nothing but a relic <note>original: "relique"; a remaining part or trace of something</note> and the remainder of

### Written to the Athenians
<page-num>55</page-num>

...smoke separated. But yet note that he was a kind of spirit before. Man was made of this dross <note>original: "drosse"; the scum or impurities left over from a refining process</note>, and is a being most subtle in spirit. Indeed, he is that very spirit—that is, a revelation or sign <margin>A twofold eternal.</margin> of a twofold Eternal: one called *Caleruthum*, the other *Meritorium*. <margin>What Caleruthum is.</margin> *Caleruth* <note>original: *Caleruthum*; a specialized mystical term likely referring to the primordial substance or the first state of the eternal</note> is a sign of discovery in the first Eternal. This seeks or desires the other, which is God. The cause of this is natural, because all things have an affinity for and struggle <margin>Like seeks its like.</margin> for that out of which they came, and they desire those natures that are nearest to them. Whatever the Creator gave or used when He made a thing, that very same thing is what the created thing earnestly desires and presses after. 

Yet we must know that the creature does not desire his Creator by nature or natural instinct, but rather seeks after that out of which it came. Thus, man's body does not desire God, but the matter out of which it was separated; for it was not taken out of God. And that matter is the life and habitation wherein the eternal *meritorium* <note>original: *Meritorium*; here referring to the divine or spiritual reward/essence dwelling within matter</note> dwells: thus everything returns to its own essence.

<unclear>... ... ...</unclear>

Now, seeing every thing is greedily desirous of its origin—that is, of the mystery out of which it proceeded—we are further to consider that that thing is everlasting life; and that which comes from there is mortal. Nevertheless, in the mortal there remains that which is eternal—namely, the soul—as may be learned elsewhere.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the fundamental nature of matter, specifically focusing on the cyclical transition between visible and invisible states.</meta>

<page-num>36</page-num>

# The third Book of Philosophy

<margin>How perishable things return to their first state.</margin>
And although perishable <note>original: "corruptible"</note> things must return to their former state, it is so that enduring things may be joined together, and thus a collection and union of things can be made. The form and substance of all things—both those that perish and those that remain—originate from the **spirit of smoke** <note>This likely refers to a philosophical or alchemical concept of a vaporous or ethereal primal state of matter.</note>, just as hail or lightning comes from a cloud. These things have a physical body, but the matter from which they originate is invisible.

<margin>All things proceed from the invisible.</margin>
We must conclude that all things proceed from the invisible, but without causing any harm or damage to it, and that matter always has the power to renew the same thing again. Because of this, the whole world passes away like a ship and returns again to the same matter of the **spirit of smoke**. It generates <note>original: "gendreth"</note> and nourishes without any tangible essence. In this respect, the original state may be brought forth a second time.

<margin>Platonic emanation</margin>
By this we also know that no creature was merely begotten, but was made and procreated <note>In this context, "procreated" refers to being brought into physical existence from a prior source rather than simple biological reproduction.</note>. For the Supreme Good <note>original: "chiefest good," likely referring to God or the Prime Mover.</note> ordained in the beginning that everything should proceed out of the invisible and be made physical; and then be separated from the body again, and so become invisible once more. Then all things are coupled and united again and brought back to the **first matter** <note>original: "prima materia," the fundamental substance from which all others are made in ancient and medieval philosophy.</note>. And though they are so united, there is still some difference and diversity among them. One receives sustenance <note>original: "entertainement"</note> from another; one gives sustenance to another. That **first matter** is the home of all things, both those that can be perceived by the senses and those that cannot. All must return to that place and condition, whether they are rational or irrational; nothing can escape this change, but shall certainly return and hasten to the dwelling from which it came.

<summary>This page describes a cyclical process of creation where physical objects emerge from an invisible "first matter" or "spirit of smoke" and eventually return to it. It argues that all substance, whether living or inanimate, originates from and ultimately retreats back into this invisible source as part of a divine order.</summary>

<keywords>first matter, spirit of smoke, Platonic emanation, corruption, procreation, metaphysics, natural philosophy, cyclical existence</keywords>

<meta>The previous page concludes the preceding section of the text. This page begins a new discourse addressed "To the Athenians," exploring the alchemical and elemental origins of physical matter. The catchword "Enur" indicates the first word of the following page.</meta>

# Written to the Athenians.

<margin>The body is a condensed smoke.</margin>
Every body or tangible substance is nothing but a condensed fume. From this, we may conclude that there are many types of coagulation: one for wood, another for stones, and a third for metals. But the physical body is nothing more than a fume, rising like smoke out of the matter or <term>matrix</term> <note>original: matrix; here meaning a womb or the fundamental environment in which something originates</note> in which it exists.

<margin>Bodies are formed, as it were, out of a fume.</margin>
Thus, everything that grows out of the earth is a fume rising out of the moisture of <term>Mercury</term> <note>In alchemy, Mercury represents the fluid, volatile, or "spirit" principle of all matter</note>, which takes many forms and sends forth various fumes for herbs, trees, and other such things. This fume—when it breaks forth from its first matter, essence, or origin, or as soon as it first breathes out of the <term>matrix</term> and touches or is stopped by the outward air—is immediately condensed. This fume then evaporates constantly and continually. As long as that driving force <note>original: appulse; a striking against or an impulsive force</note> remains warm, the thing will grow; when the boiling ceases, there is no more steam, and so the thickening and growth come to an end.

<margin>Wood originates from the smoke of Derfes.</margin>
Wood is the smoke of <term>Derfes</term> <note>A technical term used in Paracelsian alchemy to describe the elemental essence or "spiritual" matter of wood</note>. Within it lies the <term>specific</term> <note>The unique, essential substance that determines a thing's particular nature</note> matter of which it is made. Nor is it made of that fume alone, but it may also be made of some other "deric" matter.

<margin>Herbs spring from the smoke of Leffas.</margin>
In the same way, <term>Leffas</term> <note>A Paracelsian term for the vital essence or "womb" of plants</note> is the boiling matter from whose fume all herbs spring. Only Leffas, and nothing else, is the sole destiny of herbs. God is much more wonderful in these <term>specifics</term> than in all other parts of nature.

<margin>Stannar is the mother of metals.</margin>
<term>Stannar</term> <note>A Paracelsian term referring to the "mother-source" or mineral womb where metals are generated</note> is the mother of metals, which provides the prime matter to metal through its stream. Metals are nothing but thickened smoke from Stannar.

<summary>This page outlines a Paracelsian alchemical theory where all physical matter—including wood, herbs, and metals—originates as a "fume" or smoke that condenses into a solid state upon contact with air. It introduces specialized terms for the elemental sources of these materials, such as Derfes, Leffas, and Stannar.</summary>

<keywords>alchemy, Paracelsus, Athens, matrix, Mercury, Derfes, Leffas, Stannar, coagulation, elemental fumes, metaphysics</keywords>

-># The third Book of Philosophy.<-

<margin>Enur is the substance of stones.</margin>
**Enur** <term>A term in Paracelsian philosophy referring to the vaporous or spiritual essence from which minerals and stones originate.</term> is the smoke of stones. Briefly, whatever has a body is nothing but curdled smoke, in which a specific destiny <note>original: "predestination"</note> lies hidden. And all things shall at last vanish like smoke. For that specific force which causes things to solidify <note>original: "coagulate"</note> has power only for a certain appointed time. The same must be understood regarding the process of solidification itself.

<margin>Bodies vanish into smoke.</margin>
For all bodies shall pass away and vanish into nothing but smoke; they shall all end in a vapor <note>original: "fume"</note>. This is the end of physical <note>original: "corporeall"</note> things, both living and dead.

---

->### T E X T . 4 .<-

<margin>Man is composed of smoke.</margin>
Man is a solidified vapor. The solidification of the reproductive <note>original: "spermatick"</note> matter is made of nothing but the boiling vapors and reproductive parts of the body. This shall be dissolved <note>original: "resolv'd"</note> again into a similar vapor, so that the end may be as the beginning was. We see nothing in our own selves but thickened smoke, formed into a man by human destiny.

<margin>All food is but a curdled vapor.</margin>
All that we take and eat is but a thickened vapor from the bodily fluids <note>original: "humors"</note> or moisture. What we eat is consumed by the life force for this reason: so that the solid form might melt and be dissolved again, just as the Sun thaws ice so that it may pass into the air like smoke. Life consumes all things. It is a spirit that consumes all substances and physical things. Take notice here of the separation of the digested mystery: if every thing returns to its first state from which it came, then what we eat will be consumed together with our life. This is meant of those

...things that are not changed. For transmutation <note>The process of changing one substance into another, often used in alchemy to describe physical and spiritual refinement.</note> cannot be reversed or hindered. <margin>Life causes every change.</margin> Life is the cause of all transmutation. So then, transmutation is changed into the frailty of the body, but is separated again from the body. When the body decays <note>original: "putrefieth"</note>, transmutation no longer has any power; the secret nature <note>original: "mystery"</note> of that which is separated follows in the process of decay. All the properties that a man has in him—derived from herbs and other things—are separated from one another, every thing returning to its own fundamental nature <note>original: "essence"</note>. This separation is like when ten or twelve things are mixed together and then separated again, so that every one may regain its specific identity. Thus, frequent eating is nothing but a breaking down <note>original: "dissolution"</note> of bodies. From this, the matter of bodies is separated by vomiting and purging, so that it becomes nothing but a foul-smelling vapor mixed with something that is good. Nature only seeks that which is refined <note>original: "subtill"</note> or pure; it rejects the coarse <note>original: "grosse"</note> matter. Life dissolves stones, metals, the earth, and all things: they have no other way to be separated from the body except by the power of life.

## CHAPTER II.

### Where that True Medicine is to be Found.

<margin>
Wheat does not grow without a place, nor can we have meal without bran, neither is honey without a sting.

Such Secrets are regenerated without any mixture of qualities.
</margin>
**E**very thing that God created good is extremely perfect and incorruptible, as the heavens are; but whatsoever is found in these sublunary <term>sublunary|meaning "under the moon"—referring to the earthly realm which is subject to decay, unlike the eternal heavens</term> inferior things has a twofold nature: a perfect one and an imperfect one. That is to say, there is a "fifth essence" <term>fift Essence|the quintessence; the purest, most potent medicinal core of a substance</term> and there are dregs, which may be separated from one another by fire. 

Seeing, therefore, that the true medicine is wrapped up in rinds, barks, wombs <note>original: "matrixes"; the environment or vessel in which a substance is formed.</note>, receptacles, husks, garments, and dwellings—just as almonds and all kernels are covered with bark and rind (for nature does not bring forth the kernel of the chestnut without its shell and prickly husk)—it is necessary that the same must be separated from the impure elements by the artificial anatomy <note>In this context, "anatomy" refers to the chemical dissection or breaking down of a substance into its parts.</note> of chemists <note>original: "Chymiſts"; here referring to alchemists who practiced laboratory medicine.</note> before we can reach the pure medicine. For the bonds are loosened by art and industry, and the faculties of healing are set at liberty.

Therefore, in all orders of things that are cherished in the bosom of the elements—namely, in those three families of nature: the *Vegetable*, *Animal*, and *Mineral* (out of which are commonly...

-># Hermes to the Athenians.<-

<margin>Invisible things are made visible by bodies.</margin>
Although nature, as we have said, is invisible within bodies and substances, that invisibility becomes visible by means of those bodies. Just as the essence of every thing is visibly seen in its virtues <note>original: "virtues"; here meaning inherent powers or medicinal properties</note> and colors, invisible bodies have no other form but this kind of bodily consistency. Therefore, observe that invisible things contain all the elements within them and operate in every element. They can send the fire and power of their element out of themselves; they can send forth *air*, just as a man does his breath, and also *water*, as a man produces urine. They possess the nature of *earth* as well, having originated from the earth. Think of it this way: the liquid or moisture of the earth boils daily and sends upward that subtle spirit which it contained within itself. By this, invisible things and the Firmament <term>the heavens or the sky, historically viewed as a physical dome</term> itself are nourished, which cannot exist without a vapor.

<margin>How the Firmament and invisible things are nourished.</margin>
Incorporeal <term>non-physical or spiritual</term> things can no more live without food and drink than corporeal things can. Therefore, stones grow out of the earth, but they grow from a spirit that matches their own nature. Every stone draws its own spirit to itself.

<margin>The origin of fiery dragons and ghosts.</margin>
From such things come ghosts, fiery dragons, and many more. If, then, invisible things as well as visible ones are active in their essence, it is due to the nature of the "great mystery" <note>likely a reference to the *Mysterium Magnum*, a concept in alchemy and mysticism representing the primordial source of all things</note>, just as wood is set on fire by a candle or taper, which loses or wastes nothing by doing so. And though a thing may not be corporeal, it must still have something corporeal to preserve itself

# READER,

What I have done in the translation of these two remarkably eminent men, *Paracelsus* and *Crollius*, has been as a *translator* rather than an *interpreter*, so that the *author's* meaning more than my own might be searched out. Although the translation is not as elegant or expressive as the original, yet (if my judgment does not fail) the matter is preserved entire and sound. In both treatises <note>original: "Tracts"</note> you will meet with some strange <note>original: "uncouth"</note> and unusual words which, for your better understanding (as you are not acquainted with such language), I have here alphabetically explained, as follows:

---

->A<-

**ADECH** <term>Adech: A term used by Paracelsus to describe the "inner man" or the creative imagination that conceives of an action before it is physically executed.</term> is our inward and invisible man, which first shapes those things in the mind that afterward are done with the hands.

<summary>In this preface, the translator explains his commitment to a literal translation of the works of Paracelsus and Crollius and introduces an alphabetical glossary of specialized alchemical terms.</summary>

*Arcana*, secrets or mysteries. *Arcanum*, a secret; or (according to Paracelsus <note>original: Parac.</note>) the hidden incorporeal virtue in natural things.

*Archaltes*, the prop or pillars of the earth.

*Archeus*, the chief, exalted, invisible spirit: the occult virtue, artificer, and <term>physician of nature</term> in every one.

*Astra*, Stars; also the force and virtue of things produced by preparations.

---

*Bisemutum*, the palest or worst sort of lead; it is <term>tin-glass</term> <note>original: Tin-glasse; a historical name for the element Bismuth</note>.

## C

*Cabala*, that most secret knowledge, which the Hebrew Rabbis <note>original: Rabbins</note> say was given by God with the Law of Moses.

*Caleruth*, a note or sign of desire, when a thing tends toward its first matter and would return to where it came from.

*Cobaltum*, a stone from which matter is made that is useful <note>original: behoofull</note> to medicine. It is a mineral.

## D

*Derses*, a secret vapor of the earth from which wood grows.

*Durdales*, spirits that have bodies and live among trees.

## G

*Gabalam*, *Gebalum*: a thing repaired, restored, or curdled.

*Gabalis homo*: such a man.

*Gamahaa*: occurs when a living thing is affected or influenced by its physical image or representation—for example, when a pigeon is struck dead from the top of a house simply by thrusting a pin through a drawing of it on paper. <note>This refers to the concept of "Gamahé" or "Gamaheu," which were natural stones or talismans believed to have magical properties due to their shapes.</note>

*Gnomes*, *Gnomi*: these are little men, dwarfs, or rather spirits with physical bodies that live under the earth; pygmies who are scarcely half a foot high. <note>In Paracelsian philosophy, gnomes are the elemental spirits of the earth.</note>

*Gonetick*. <note>The original text provides no definition for this term here; it likely refers to "gonetic," relating to the generative or reproductive powers.</note>

## H

*Hilech*: the "star of medicine" <note>original Latin: *astrum medicine*</note>, or the spirit hidden within a medicine.

## I

*Iliaster*: the first matter of all things, consisting of salt, sulfur, and mercury. Generally, it is understood to be the hidden <term>occult</term> virtue of nature, through which all things increase, grow, multiply, and are nourished. See the *Chemical Lexicon* <note>original Latin: *Vid. Lex. Chym.*</note>. <note>Iliaster is a term coined by Paracelsus to describe primeval matter or the foundation of all physical life.</note>

# L

<term>*Limboan*,</term> otherwise known as <term>*Lymbus*,</term> is the primary matter or "seed" of the world and all things within it. <note>In Paracelsian philosophy, the Lymbus is the primordial chaos from which God created the universe.</note>

<term>*Lorind*,</term> is the movement of the waters accompanied by a musical noise; it is considered a sign of some imminent change.

*Sylvesters*, airy men or airy spirits living in woods and groves.
*Sirens* <note>original: "Syrenes"</note>, sea-monsters born of the Nymphs.

# T

*Talcum*, a bright, clear substance from which oil of **Talc** is made. There are four types of **Talc**, regarding which you may see the *Lexicon Chymicum* <note>original: "Lex. Chym."; likely referring to the *Lexicon Alchemiae* (1612) by Martin Ruland the Elder, a standard dictionary of alchemical terms</note>.

*Trarames*, the actions of the spirits and ghosts of dead men, which are heard but not seen.

*Tronum*, celestial dew made from the air.

*Truphat*, the occult <note>hidden or secret</note> virtue of minerals, which promotes the development of every metal.

*Tura.*

*Turban*, an innumerable multitude of stars in the firmament of heaven; it also refers to a presage <note>a sign or omen</note> from all things contained within the fourfold inferior world of the elements.

# V

*Umbratiles* <note>derived from the Latin *umbra*, meaning "shadow"</note>, bodies that have once rotted and were afterward made visible again by the stars through a magical virtue.

*Undena*, airy men and earthly spirits.

*Woarchadumie.* <note>Likely a variant of "Voarchadumia," a term coined by the alchemist Giovanni Pantheus to describe a "cabala of metals" or a specialized alchemical process for refining gold.</note>

# Z

*Zonnetes*, fantastical <term>supernatural or illusory</term> bodies of the Gnomes <note>In the philosophy of Paracelsus, gnomes are elemental spirits associated with the earth.</note>.

So it is that now, through reproduction, two men are always born: a corporeal, elemental, visible man—the organ and instrument of the invisible—and an incorporeal <term>astral man</term> <note>original: "Astrallick man"</note> who moves, guides, and performs all skillful matters. For the stars now within man always produce those two during generation. The visible elementary body of flesh and blood is formed in the mother's womb out of the four <term>elements</term>. However, the invisible sidereal body, which is capable of attaining <term>philosophy</term> <note>here meaning deep natural or occult wisdom</note>, is from the stars of the firmament. 

For that "little world," man, is one and the same in all things with his parent, the "great world." But just as the great world is distinguished by its shell or outside from the angelic world, so man, the little world, is distinguished by his skin or outside from the great world.

Hence it is that the sidereal, internal, <term>Olympic</term> <note>celestial or heavenly</note>, incorporeal, or <term>Gabalic man</term> <note>original: "Gabalis Homo"; likely referring to the "Caballine" or spiritual essence of man in esoteric tradition</note>—this "coagulated" or curdled man—is the same as the firmament of the stars, as has been often said; just as redness is one with wine, or whiteness with snow,

In them, the harmony, properties, and affinities that these magnetic virtues have between the spirit and sidereal body of Man and the stars are made manifest.

<margin>
Wisdom is the beginning of enchantment; the stars <note>original: "Astra's"</note> do those things which human wisdom desires. In respect to the elementary body, it is a spirit and performs all spiritual operations. Heaven knows all things most certainly; all actions and events of men are pictured in the stars, and every living creature has its ascendant sign in heaven, as does also the brutish man. Every body proceeds from an invisible and incomprehensible subsisting spirit. The <unclear>S...</unclear> is a threefold
</margin>

—namely, the spirit of perpetual motion of invisible nature, or the magnetic spark of the soul of the world—lives and works effectively. All animal wisdom, craftsmanship, arts, sciences, and the knowledge of all things lie hidden in the stars <note>original: "Astra's"</note> of the firmament <term>firmament: the sphere of the stars or the heavens</term>. 

There is nothing so hidden in the world which is not prefigured in the stars. Indeed, all the stars of the firmament, which are the tincture <term>tincture: a spiritual or medicinal essence or quality</term> of our mind's contemplation, can—by their own innate or natural force and by imagining—produce bodily and visible things out of that which does not appear. For example, in a clear sky a great cloud suddenly arises, from which come rain, mud, snow, dew, thunder, and hail. Though they were nothing before production, yet being produced out of invisible things, they become great bodies.

From this we may observe that all things in the first Creation were produced out of the **DIVINE NOTHING**, or invisible **Cabalistical Point** <note>original: "Caba-listicall Poynt"; likely referring to the concept of the 'Ein Sof' in Jewish mysticism, the infinite void from which God emanates creation</note>, into something. This God did in a moment, for his works cannot be delayed by time. All things proceeded out of the invisible darkness and were called out to the visible light by the **WORD** speaking and the Spirit nurturing. 

Now, since man had his **sidereal body** <term>sidereal: relating to the stars or constellations</term> from the stars of the firmament, and the whole imagination of man depends on the stars of the firmament—indeed, is the same and remains one with them—it must be that the firmament also has an imagination. However, the firmament's imagination is without reason, whereas man, the offspring of the world, possesses reason.

...the thing conceived by the attractive force of the imagination, so that it may produce the "Architect of the Imagination" as an internal spirit from within itself. <margin>Magic or Faith, by transplanting minds, has power over all spirits and planetary influences <note>original: "Ascendants," referring to the astrological signs rising on the eastern horizon at a specific time.</note>.</margin>

For example, just as the sun’s beams are directed to us with their heat through a magnifying glass <note>original: "burning Glasse"</note>, the imagination can produce whatever we see with our eyes in the greater world <note>The macrocosm or the universe at large.</note>. Thus, through the imagination and true **Kabbalah** <note>original: "Gabalia"; refers to a form of Jewish mysticism adapted by Renaissance thinkers into "Christian Cabala" to understand the relationship between the divine and the world.</note>, all herbs, all growing things, and all metals may be produced. This branch of magic is called Kabbalistic, and it is supported by three pillars:

First, by **TRUE PRAYERS** made in spirit and truth, when God and the created spirit are united in the *Holy of Holies* <note>The innermost sanctuary of the Temple; here used metaphorically for the deepest part of the human soul.</note>. This occurs when God is prayed to in the internal spirit—not with the noise of words, but in a sacred silence, without opening the mouth or groaning.

Secondly, by **NATURAL FAITH**, or innate wisdom, which God the Father communicated equally to all people at Creation, as his own proper flock and common inheritance <note>original: "patrimony"</note>.

Thirdly, by a strongly exalted **IMAGINATION**. The Light of Nature <note>A philosophical term referring to the innate cognitive power or divine spark that allows humans to understand the world and spiritual truths without external revelation.</note> reveals how great and how wonderful its strength and force truly are—

<meta>This page continues a discussion on the power of the human imagination and its ability to affect the physical world and the spiritual state of the individual.</meta>

<margin>
Genesis 30:37, etc.
</margin>

Nature clearly shows this, both in **Jacob's** rods mentioned by **Moses** <note>In Genesis 30, Jacob used peeled rods to influence the coloring of Laban's livestock</note> and in pregnant women who leave a mark on their child of the thing they long for, as has been previously mentioned. The imagination or fantasy of man is like a lodestone <note>original: "Load stone"; a naturally magnetized mineral</note> in its nature, attracting the imagination of other men, as we see in those who gape and yawn.

A powerful imagination causes not only a transformation <note>original: "transmutation"</note> of one's own body, but sometimes also that of another through imitation. This occurs by a certain kind of power <note>original: "Vertue"</note> that the likeness of a thing has over the thing being changed, which is moved by a powerful imagination. This appears in cases of astonishment or fainting, in the grinding or creaking of teeth, or in grating one piece of iron against another, which sets the teeth on edge. In the same way, yawning triggers yawning.

Many people, through their melancholy imagination and lack of confidence <note>original: "diffidence"</note>, have exposed themselves to the temptation of the unclean spirit, and sometimes have been overcome by it.

<margin>
True faith is the cure for a false imagination. Many fall sick and recover again through the faith of imagination.
</margin>

Many others, through their focused imagination and without doubting their own weakness, have prevailed. By a constant and firm faith in God, a mind lifted to the highest state, certain hope, and constant, passionate prayers, they have so prevailed that they have suddenly become temples of the living God.

The summary of it all is that we should worship God devoutly in honesty and holiness, as the more secret <term>Theosophers</term> <note>those who seek direct, mystical knowledge of the divine</note> or those who are "wise-hearted toward God" well know.

## CHAPTER II.

### Where True Medicine Is to Be Found.

<margin>
Wheat does not grow without a place, nor can we have meal without bran, nor is honey without a sting. Such secrets are regenerated without any combination <note>original: "complexion"; referring to the balance of qualities or humors</note> of qualities.
</margin>
**E**very thing that God created good is extremely perfect and incorruptible, such as the heavens. But whatever exists in these earthly <note>original: "sublunary"; meaning literally "below the moon"</note>, inferior things has a twofold nature: a perfect and an imperfect one. That is to say, a fifth essence <note>original: "fift Essence"; the quintessence, or the purest, most concentrated part of a substance</note> and the dregs, which may be separated from one another by fire. Since, therefore, the true medicine <note>original: "Phy-sick"</note> is wrapped up in rinds, barks, matrices <note>the natural environments or tissues in which a substance is formed</note>, receptacles, husks, garments, and coverings—just as almonds and all kernels are covered with bark and rind (for Nature does not bring forth the kernel of the chestnut without a shell and prickly husk)—it is necessary that the medicine be separated from the impure elements by the artificial anatomy <note>here referring to chemical dissection or laboratory separation</note> of chemists <note>original: "Chymists"; those practicing the art of alchemy and early chemistry</note> before we can reach the pure medicine. For the bonds are loosened by art and industry, and the healing faculties are set at liberty.

Therefore, in all orders of things that are cherished in the bosom of the elements—namely, in those three families of nature: the *Vegetable*, *Animal*, and *Mineral* (out of which are commonly

(commonly had medicines enough to preserve health and cure all bodily diseases), there is contained that True and specific medicine <term>Physick: an archaic term for the practice of medicine or a medicinal remedy</term> against all ailments <term>distempers: physical illnesses or imbalances of the body</term>. As has been said, this does not consist in the four outward, naked, superficial, and material qualities (as *Theophrastus* <note>original: *Theophrastus* refers to the Swiss physician and alchemist Paracelsus, born Philippus Aureolus Theophrastus Bombastus von Hohenheim</note> learnedly discourses). Instead, it is a certain specific virtue <term>virtue: the inherent power or medicinal property of a substance</term> concealed in the very seeds. This power proceeds naturally without human art; the Creator—that great Master-Worker of all things—planted it in every growing thing at the beginning of Creation by the power of that omnipotent *Word*, through which all things were brought out of darkness into light.

<margin>Heaven is the builder of the external structures, not of the secrets, wonders, and mysteries that dwell within them.</margin>
The faculties and virtues with which mixed bodies are endowed (like the soul in a human body from the beginning of Creation) do not come from the outside. They are not infused into them by a momentary position of the stars, nor are they made up of a chance meeting of atoms. These virtues do not proceed from the physical body, nor the mixture of the body or its visible form; if they did, they could not be separated without destroying or corrupting that body and form. We see this in cinnamon and pepper, whose virtues are extracted either by human art or vanish through age and long storage. For just as all natural actions proceed from spirits and spiritual tinctures <term>tinctures: in alchemy, the essential essence or spiritual principle of a substance</term>, in which the mechanical sciences of the Three Principles <note>original: *Three principles* refers to the Paracelsian alchemical triad of Salt, Sulfur, and Mercury, which were believed to be the base components of all matter</note> have their strength; so the process of healing proceeds from the spirits and vital tinctures of those spirits, not from the physical bodies or dead, superficial <note>original: *Relloceous* or *Relollaceous* is likely an archaic or specialized term referring to the husks or material remains of a substance</note> qualities.

Through a specific and *harmonious* virtue, these things serve to cure and comfort the heart, and so on. Likewise, among *minerals*, there are *magical* and *hieroglyphical characters* <note>hieroglyphical characters: likely referring to the "signatures" or natural markings on stones and minerals that were believed to indicate their medicinal uses</note> which the wise thinkers of *antiquity* attributed to them, without being reckless or superstitious.

Whether an herb or a word heals, it is by the natural power <note>original: "Vertue"</note> given by God—specifically, by the Spirit of God made one with nature through his word, *Fiat* <note>Latin: "Let it be done," referring to the divine command in Genesis.</note>. 

<margin>Paracelsus <note>Philippus Aureolus Theophrastus Bombastus von Hohenheim (1493–1541), a famous Swiss physician and alchemist.</note> says that magical characters are the compounds and syrups of spirits.</margin>

Physicians have also performed great cures through the *Created Word*, or incarnate mercy. All these things are done solely by the efficacy of the *Triune and Divine Word*, which heals and preserves all things. 

<margin>Luke 11:14.</margin>

We see this in the miracles of our Savior; when he restored the deaf and dumb (whom all the pills and syrups in all the shops of the world could not help), he did it not through natural means, but through himself. He did it with *one word*, and he is that *Word*—that is, the uncreated <note>original: "increated"</note> mercy of God. 

<margin>John 1:3.</margin>
<margin>Not by bread alone (Matthew 4:4).</margin>

Through him all things were created, and from him all herbal remedies <note>original: "simples," referring to medicinal plants consisting of only one ingredient</note> flow; he also works daily with the Father in all things. Whatever power or operation exists within creatures—both in the macrocosm <note>original: "the great world"</note> and the microcosm <note>original: "the little world"</note>—all of it is certainly produced by God incarnate.

# CHAPTER III.

## How the True <term>Medicine</term> <note>original: "Physick"</note>, which is covered by bark and rind, is to be extracted and rightly prepared by Fire.

<margin>The <term>Physician</term> <note>original: "Physiti-an"</note> perfects God's creation by Fire.</margin>
<margin>Ecclesiasticus 39:26, etc.</margin>

All things in respect of their <term>original substance</term> <note>original: "first matter"</note> were created perfect, but the <term>chemist</term> <note>original: "Chymist"</note> perfects the final product through Fire. This is because nothing in this lower world (which is subject to the cycles of <term>birth and decay</term> <note>original: "generation and corruption"</note>) is so noble that it does not contain poison within itself, and in relation to another thing, this poison is very close to its essential healing power or <term>medicine</term> <note>original: "Physick"</note>. In all the primary works of God, where there is harm, there is also help; where there is venom, there is virtue. Therefore, nothing was created in vain, but all things were made for some use. For Nature has so ordained that Good and Evil, which the <term>earthly elements</term> <note>original: "sublunary Elements"</note> produce, should always be joined together in all things, to remind us daily of the fear of God.

As soon as the Almighty gave power to man, He immediately raised up an enemy, lest the

Analysis and vital anatomy of bodies (as has been said) is possible because there is no constant quality of any body which is not found either in the Salt, or Mercury, or Sulfur of that same body.

For in the workshop of nature, there is animal, vegetable, and mineral salt; animal, vegetable, and mineral sulfur; and vegetable, animal, and mineral mercury.

The first state of things was pure, sound, and perfect, without corruption or death. For the great and all-working God, for the sake of his infinite glory, created all things good by his will, so that all things might glorify him and live holily and incorruptibly according to the prescribed order.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the fallen state of nature and the necessity of alchemical study to overcome the impurities introduced after the Fall of Man. Catchword: and.</meta>

<page-num>96</page-num>

<margin>
Inla’s temperament <note>original: κρᾶσις (krasis), meaning a mixing or blending of qualities or humors</note>.
The transformation <note>original: transplantation</note> of the human being occurs through the misfortune and onset of sickness upon it. Since the Fall, we carry in our own bosom the enemy of both our creation and our reproduction, which causes sickness and death through innate conflict and corruption.
</margin>

Impure influences <note>original: Tinctures</note> (even infinite evils) entered in; by their mixture with the miserable state of our lives, along with that troublesome companion, the world, the beauty of the entire created being was altered. Impurity was joined to the pure roots, which predetermined the existence of diseases. For the roots of sicknesses in certain individuals or species do not exist separately on their own; instead, they are implanted and mixed within the pure, original seeds of things. The nourishment of natural things, however, comes from the fruits of those seeds which spring up in the four "wombs" or Elements <note>the four classical elements: Earth, Water, Air, and Fire</note>.

Therefore, nature as it exists now gives us nothing in the world that is pure, but has mixed all things with many impurities. As if by the spur of necessity, this often reminds us that we should begin to learn the knowledge of chemistry <note>original: Chymistry, referring here to alchemical science and the purification of substances</note> from our cradles. So long as we are shut out of Paradise and living in the suburbs of this world, we ought to till and cultivate the EARTH—that is, the entire framework of the world—by marveling at it, searching into it, and knowing both the visible and invisible primordial clay <note>original: *Limus* (Latin), referring to the "dust of the ground" from which man was formed in Genesis</note> of the Earth. We should labor to obtain our bread and other necessities for this present life as nature’s laborers—not lazily, but by the sweat of our brows. In this way, by taking up the cross which we should bear with patience, it might stir up our industry in this **LAND of LABOR** to attain the fruits of earthly and heavenly wisdom, lest base and sluggish idleness cause us to waste away.

In its own way, every creature is good; though it may be poison to man, it is often common food for another creature. Spiders are good for hens and sparrows; toads for serpents; and serpents for stags and storks. However, these forms of medicine <note>original: "Phyfick"</note> work better when they are extracted, rather than while they are "drowned" in matter, which always hinders and restrains the power and operation of the secret <note>the hidden medicinal property</note>.

->Hence many advantages follow.<-

<margin>Reasons why alchemical medicine <note>original: "Spagyrick Physick"</note> duly prepared is to be preferred over other irregular <note>original: "Hetroclite"</note> mixtures found in shops.</margin>
First, because many sick people will take it more easily and willingly. Some are so unwilling that they would rather die than drink such a large amount of those muddy and swamp-like <note>original: "pudly"</note> potions. Such potions spoil a person's complexion; even the physician who prescribed them and the apothecary who mixed them would abhor and refuse to drink them in a similar situation.

Secondly, although these medicines are often used, they do not hurt the stomach. Nothing hinders them from doing their work; they quickly affect the body and are processed by it. Because they are already refined <note>original: "separate"</note> in the stomach, they are carried by a natural force without hindrance to their intended destinations <note>original: "known lodgings"</note>. This ensures that harsh and earthy components do not stick to the internal organs, which might otherwise cause ulcers or make frequent users sickly—as often happens with the common use of ordinary <note>original: "vulgar"</note> medicines.

Thirdly, because all harmful qualities are either completely cast out or easily suppressed in these **essences** by the mixing <note>original: "permixture"</note> of other most exquisite properties. It cannot be denied that this **alchemical** <note>original: "Spagyrick"</note> art is so necessary that no one can be a safe physician without it. For many times within a single herb or substance <note>original: "simple thing"</note>, there are different components, sometimes with contrary qual- <note>The text continues on the next page with "ities."</note>

<meta>The text continues from the previous page, completing the word "qualities."</meta>

...qualities, of which one may be harmful and the rest healthful. We see this in <term>opium</term> and <term>honey</term>, which can only be understood by the separation of their substances—a task that cannot be done without this Art <note>Referring to the "Art" of Chemistry or Alchemy.</note>. By this Art alone, the <term>Galenists</term> <note>Followers of the traditional medical system of Galen, which relied on the balance of four "humors."</note> may prove their axioms. They affirm that all bitter things are "hot" in nature; yet, while <term>opium</term> is exceedingly bitter, its <term>numbing power</term> <note>original: "stupefactive vertue"</note> is predominant. <term>Roses</term> also, and <term>succory</term> <note>Chicory.</note>, are bitter, yet they are applied as cooling treatments. The knot of this contradiction must be cut with an <term>anatomical knife</term>—which is <term>fire</term> <note>A metaphor for using heat to break down and analyze chemical components.</note>. For by this means, we come to understand the temperament of individual ingredients by separating their substances. Thus, we find that in <term>opium</term> there is a sweet <term>narcotic sulfur</term> <note>In alchemical theory, "sulfur" referred to the flammable, oily essence of a substance.</note> and a bitter, hot <term>salt</term> <note>In alchemy, "salt" referred to the solid, earthy principle left after combustion.</note> that penetrates through subtle dissolution and causes sweating without any numbing effect.

<margin>Poison reduced to its <term>secret essence</term> <note>original: "Arcanum."</note> is not poison, but a sovereign medicine; thus the "earthly <term>planets</term>" <note>An alchemical term for metals, which were each associated with a specific planet (e.g., Lead with Saturn).</note> are healed of their "leprosy" <note>Impurities or corrosion in metals.</note>, and stinking smells are turned into sweet fragrances through chemical digestion.</margin>

And it is much to be admired that, though the poison of metals is most deadly, it may be so corrected and improved by the help of this Art and of <term>fire</term> that it may be taken into the whole body without danger. Skillful physicians know this well, for they know how to pick and cull the best things out of the worst. This is plain by the example of <term>arsenic</term> <note>original: "Arseck."</note>, which, despite its unruly malice, is tamed artificially with <term>saltpeter</term> <note>Potassium nitrate, used here as a purifying agent.</note> through the assistance of <term>fire</term>.

<term>Minerals</term>, whose spirits exceed ours in refinement, and precious stones, ought to be considered medicinal; when rightly prepared, these greatly excel all vegetables <note>Plant-based medicines.</note> in effecting cures.

<meta>This page continues an argument regarding the medicinal superiority of minerals and metals over plant-based substances.</meta>

**First**, because so great a power <note>original: "vertue"</note> and such strong effects cannot be imprinted upon such soft matter as that of plants and living things, so as to retain and hold such an impression. Nor can it be that vegetables, which are prone to decay <note>original: "corruption"</note>, should so free the human body from decay as the spirits of perfect metals <note>In alchemy, "perfect metals" typically refers to gold and silver, which were believed to be immune to rust or rot</note> do, which are not subject to decay.

**Secondly**, it is clear that minerals and imperfect metals are endowed with great medicinal virtues, as appears in surgical <note>original: "Chyrurgical"</note> medicines, there being scarcely any ointment prepared that is not made of an imperfect metal or mineral. Therefore, perfect things have undoubtedly received far greater force and powers from the Almighty.

**Thirdly**, because Nature—which desires to bring forth living things and plants fit not for just one action alone but for many functions and performances—could by no means so balance the mixture of those bodies that they should attain such admirable power and allow for the solid and stable nature of a balsam <note>a healing substance, often used in this context to mean a preservative that prevents bodily decay</note>.

**Fourthly**, because stones require a long time for their generation, but perfect bodies are generated in a shorter span. Nature, therefore, through the length of time, can adorn precious stones and other metallic <note>original: "metallary"</note> bodies with a greater capacity for working, for they are not distracted by a variety of sensory and mobile functions <note>original: "offices"</note>.

**Add also**, that precious stones are more to be commended than others for their excellent temperament and splendor, such as in the *Bohemian*

<meta>This page continues a discussion on the philosophical and chemical nature of matter, specifically addressing the "spirit" found in all things and the limitations of ancient medical authorities like Galen regarding chemical separation.</meta>

...that the things of Nature are so tucked together <note>original: "coucht"</note> by a subtle artist, that in everything there is everything potentially, though it does not actually appear.

But I refrain from citing more authors, though I might produce a great heap of *philosophers* who confirm these things—not by common and superficial <note>original: "outside"</note> arguments, but by solid reasons drawn from the inner nature of things. *Book 1, chapter 19.* Whey, butter, and cheese are the three primary components of milk, for all earthiness <note>original: "Terresterity"</note> is salt.

Moreover, the chemical way of refining <note>original: "Subtilisting"</note>, extracting, and separating being imperfect, it was not much used in *Galen's* days (for they knew not how to separate the bark and husk from the kernel!). It was altogether unknown to him; yet, despite being ignorant of it, he very much desired it, as may be gathered from his own words. He says that he tried all ways and means to distinguish the faculties of simple medicines and discern the hot parts of vinegar from the cold—if only he might find some method to separate the contrary parts of vinegar as well as those of milk. In this matter, he might have obtained his desire if he had been well-skilled in the <term>distillation art</term> <note>original: "Distillatory Art"; the chemical process of purifying a liquid by successive evaporation and condensation</note>. Nor is it any disgrace to *Hippocrates* or *Galen* that they did not know these things. For **God** and **Nature** (which is the order and series of God's works, which obey the power, word, and command of **God**, and borrow all their virtue and efficacy from him) do nothing in vain.
