## CHAPTER 4.

### How, and by what virtue <note>original: "Vertue," meaning the inherent power or efficacy</note>, medicine <note>original: "Phyſick"</note> works upon the human body and cures its diseases.

<margin>In the book *On Winds* <note>original Latin: *In lib. de flatibus*, a treatise attributed to Hippocrates</note></margin>
What **physicians** commonly debate and argue about is **whether** diseases are to be cured by **CONTRARIES** <note>original: "CONTRARYES"</note>, according to **Hippocrates's maxim**, or by **LIKE** with **LIKE**, according to **Paracelsus**.

**Note** that both of these **maxims** may be accepted within the **anatomy of nature**, even though they seem to contradict one another. In this way, many people fail to understand the **intent** <note>original: "Mind"</note> of **philosophers**, which seems contradictory to them because they do not know how to reconcile these ideas through a timely and appropriate interpretation. For **medicine** is nothing more than the application of what is needed—namely, a refreshing of the body's strength and **balsam** <note>In this context, "balsam" refers to the body's natural internal preservative or healing essence</note>, and the removal of the excesses <note>original: "superfluityes"</note> or impurities that cause diseases. ***Paracelsus***, therefore, does not find fault with ***Hippocrates*** for saying that hunger is to be cured with meat, thirst with drink, and fullness with eva-
<margin><insert>contrast</insert></margin>

<summary>This page addresses the fundamental debate in early modern medicine regarding whether diseases are cured by opposites (Hippocrates) or by similarities (Paracelsus), suggesting that both principles can be reconciled.</summary>
<keywords>Hippocrates, Paracelsus, medicine, contraries, like cures like, balsam, medical philosophy, anatomy of nature</keywords>

...emaciation <note>The text begins mid-word; the context suggests "emaciation," the state of being abnormally thin.</note>; <term>inanition</term> <note>original: "inanition"; exhaustion caused by lack of nourishment or emptiness.</note> with <term>repletion</term> <note>original: "repletion"; the state of being excessively full, such as from overeating.</note>, labor with rest, idleness with labor; and generally, that contraries cure contraries. But he is point-blank against *Galen* <note>A prominent Greek physician (129–216 AD) whose theories dominated Western medicine for centuries.</note>, who applied that "contrariety" of *Hippocrates* <note>The "Father of Medicine" (c. 460 – c. 370 BC); the author argues Galen misinterpreted him.</note> chiefly to those bare and naked qualities which *Hippocrates* utterly disclaimed. For he, by an unhappy mistake, referred the first and principal ideas of cures to refrigerations <note>cooling</note>, calefactions <note>heating</note>, humectations <note>moistening</note>, and exsiccations <note>drying</note>, with their companions.

The only and alone NATURES of physical medicines, as has been said, or those Hippocratic virtues, are they which do the cures; the physician is but the minister or servant. And this very selfsame Nature, which is our Life and balm <note>original: "Balsom"; a healing or soothing substance believed to exist within the body.</note>, or balsamic <term>mummy</term> <note>In Paracelsian medicine, "mummy" refers to a vital spiritual essence or life-force that preserves the living body from decay.</note> that preserves the body from all corruption by means of the saline moisture—that is, of the inferior balm springing up in the inferior from the superior. I say, this very Nature of ours (which sometimes works wonders when the physicians, to their great disgrace and the shame of their profession, could do no good but have left their patient to their <term>prognostics</term> <note>predictions about the course and outcome of a disease.</note>) is its own proper physician in the human body. It asks nothing of the outward physician but <term>instauration</term> <note>restoration or renewal.</note>, or as it is commonly called, fortification applied to the diseased part by a most pure medicine when a similar Nature is not at hand. And thus the medicinal balm—like a <term>coadjutor</term> <note>an assistant or helper.</note> or privy counselor—assists the vital or radical and natural balm because of the sympathy and common agreement between them.

<margin>Nature cures with like things.</margin>
...them. In this way, it recovers the natural, decayed strength. Once restored, this strength is able, like an inward and unknown antidote, to chase all its enemies out of its kingdom <note>original: "Monarchy"</note> by the power of the <term>vital faculties</term> <note>Vital faculties: The natural powers or energies within a living body that maintain life and health.</note> alone.

To attempt to cure a disease with contrary qualities is to raise and stir up internal <note>original: "intrinsick"</note> commotions, leading to the utter overthrow of Nature, which is already too much weakened and wasted by internal <note>original: "intestine"</note> quarrels.

Furthermore, contraries will not willingly accept <note>original: "entertaine"</note> each other. If they do not join together, work upon one another, or allow themselves to be affected by each other, then where there is no true action and reaction <note>original: "passion"</note>, no true natural effect can follow.

Therefore, medicines should not be contrary to the afflicted <note>original: "grieved"</note> part, but very agreeable to it. They should have the same external nature—because of the harmony between the great and little world—as the affected place has internally. This allows the internal nature, which stands in need of help, to be both strengthened and assisted by the abundance of that external nature.

Man is therefore called a <term>microcosm</term> <note>Microcosm: Literally "little world"; the idea that the human body reflects the structure and processes of the entire universe.</note> or little world, because the whole world preserves, nourishes, and cures him. When the "fruits" of the Earth, Air, Water, and Fire within the <term>microcosm</term> are sick, they must be restored by similar "fruits" from the <term>macrocosm</term> <note>Macrocosm: The "great world" or the universe as a whole.</note>. In this way, Nature strengthens and helps its own nature. For Nature, when strengthened and assisted by its own nature, more forcibly drives out all its [enemies...]

enemies, seeing that every Nature is naturally the best preserver of itself. Thus, Nature is not only our companion, but our friend and ready helper. It alone is the genuine physician of all diseases, as *Galen* <note>original: "witnesseth"; Galen (AD 129–c. 216) was a highly influential Greek physician whose theories dominated Western medicine for over a millennium.</note> witnesses in the 13th book of his *Method*. It is the "First Mover" of every cure, without whose strength and vigor all medicine <note>original: "Physick"</note> is in vain and to no purpose. Nature, maintained in its proper balance <note>original: "Temperature"</note>, is medicinal in itself; it heals its own infirmities by its innate <term>Mummy</term> <note>In early modern medicine, "Mummy" referred to a vital life-force or internal balsam believed to exist in all living bodies that allowed them to heal themselves.</note>. When that inward nature is not the medicine, all diseases prove fatal.

It is well enough known that everything, by a kind of natural instinct, desires to be perfected and preserved. On the contrary, it most vehemently abhors its own destruction and desires as much as possible to be kept from it. Daily experience makes this plain, as when any part of our body is wounded with a sword or other weapon: for those who are wounded perceive immediate <note>original: "presentaneous"</note> relief <note>original: "succour"</note> from Nature, as if someone were hastening to help, and she is so unwearied that she will never rest until she has first cured the ugly wound and restored the wounded part to its former health <note>original: "soundnesse"</note>. 

And while some say that "contraries are to be cured by contraries" <note>The principle of *contraria contrariis curantur*, a traditional medical theory suggesting that a disease should be treated with its opposite (e.g., cooling a fever).</note>, they are not much mistaken if they do not look at the qualities, but rather the virtues of a contrary Nature. For there are as many opposing and hurtful virtues to nature as there are good and healthful ones for it. The goodness of these latter virtues is always focused on, and consists in, the preservation of Nature, just as the continual malice of the others is to

...the destruction of Nature. If they therefore intended to destroy it, those medicines are sent to help struggling Nature so that, by their goodness, they may preserve the twofold goodness of Nature, but chase out and overcome the malice of the other. Thus, the opposing and contrary virtues that are harmful to Nature are defeated and driven out by the adverse and contrary virtues within Nature. However, contrary qualities are not rooted out by contrary qualities; instead, they are irritated and provoked to conflict with each other. Through this discord, Nature is weakened more than strengthened, because Nature is not a "quality" but a "virtue" <note>In early medicine, a "virtue" refers to an essential healing power or spiritual essence, whereas a "quality" refers to physical properties like heat or cold</note>, and she chooses to be assisted by virtues rather than qualities when she seeks to successfully prevail and fight against her cruel enemy. For it is not the physician who hunts out the disease, but Nature herself (who is the "Internal Mummy" <note>original: "Internall Mummy"; a Paracelsian term for the innate vital force or "balsam" that protects the body from decay and heals wounds</note> or inward balsam) who expels all illness contrary to herself. When her own inward strength fails, she is to be supplied with outward help by her servant, the physician.

<margin>
The inward physician does the work when the human physician fails.
</margin>

Although sometimes the best medicine is to use no medicine at all, but to leave the operation to the "Archaeus" <note>original: "Archæus"; the vital principle or "inner chemist" believed to govern the body's functions and healing</note> or the art of Nature, for the nature of the inner body cures more diseases than the physician does with all his medicines. Therefore, if anyone is saved in a raging plague <note>original: "pestilence"</note> by opium—which is classified as extremely "cold"—it is not because of the coldness of the opium, but by the specific poisonous virtue of the opium, which possesses a greater degree of poison than the specific poisonous power of that plague. <note>The author argues that like cures like—a stronger "poisonous" virtue in the medicine overcomes the "poison" of the disease—rather than the traditional Galenic method of curing by opposites, such as using cold to treat a "hot" fever.</note>

<summary>This page argues that healing is performed by Nature's internal forces (the Archaeus and Internal Mummy) rather than the physician's medicines. It asserts that diseases are cured by "virtues" and "essences" rather than the physical "qualities" of heat or cold.</summary>

--- Page 176 ---
<lang>English</lang>
<page-num>119</page-num>

In this way, Nature destroys one poison with another. She overcomes a weak evil with a stronger one and fights against her enemy using both healthy and harmful weapons. Her goal is to keep her own domain safe and drive the enemy out of her territory by any means possible. Just as winter does not destroy summer, nor summer winter, but each gives way to the other, so one quality does not destroy another. Without its inherent virtue <note>original: "vertue"; here meaning the essential power or medicinal property of a substance</note>, a quality is dead and entirely accidental; consequently, it cannot provide the life or substance that a medicine must necessarily provide if it is to truly help Nature.

It is also worth noting that the "roots" of diseases in the human body are neither hot nor cold. However, since nothing can exist without heat, a disease is also described as being either hot or cold, even though these "accidents" <note>original: "Accidents"; in a philosophical sense, these are non-essential characteristics or symptoms</note> and "excrements" <note>original: "Excrements"; referring here to the waste products or physical outflows of a disease</note> are merely signs of the illness and not the disease itself. For the most harmful diseases—the traitors of the body—do not originate from the body's physical matter or from the four humors <note>The "four humors" (blood, phlegm, yellow bile, and black bile) were the basis of ancient Greek and medieval medicine</note>. Instead, they come from the nature of the "seed," or the <term>Astras</term> <note>original: "Astra's"; referring to the "stars" or celestial influences within the body</note> and invisible <term>mechanic spirits</term> <note>Spirits that function like craftsmen to build or shape the body's internal structures</note> of the <term>Three Principles</term> <note>In Paracelsian alchemy, these are Salt, Sulfur, and Mercury</note>. These spirits also build their outward "house" and habitation using physical "shells." 

These "forgers" and invisible <term>Astras</term> of diseases were unknown to the ancient doctors. <term>Physic</term> <note>original: "Phyſick"; meaning the art of healing or medicine itself</note> is a spirit, not a physical body, which only the "magician" or wise man can perceive. Therefore, the physical "body" or "earth" of <term>simples</term> <note>Medicinal herbs or individual ingredients used in a remedy</note> should be discarded, and only its "virtue," "heaven," or <term>Astrum</term> <note>The celestial or spiritual essence of a plant or mineral</note> should be kept. Mercury may be exalted <note>original: "ex-alted"; raised in intensity or potency</note> above its natural degree in three ways: by the heat of an accidental digestive virtue <note>a temporary power or quality of the digestive system</note>, by the heat of exercise and labor, or by heat proceeding from the stars <note>original: "Astra's"</note>. 

By the heat of digestion, it is distilled, and so causes all sorts of apoplexies <note>original: "Apoplexies"; sudden loss of consciousness or strokes</note>. By the heat of exercise, it is sublimed <note>converted from a solid to a vapor by heat</note>, which results in madness and frenzy. By the heat of the stars, it is precipitated, and then causes gout in the hands and feet, etc., resulting from eating and drinking things that have too much tartar <note>original: "Tattar"; in Paracelsian medicine, tartar referred to stony deposits or metabolic waste that formed "crusts" or stones in the body</note> in them. 

Those diseases that are bred of salt, he calls "saline" and "nitrous." Salt destroys health in four ways: by resolution <note>dissolving into liquid</note>, calcination (losing its liquid and humid temperament <note>the natural balance of moisture in the body</note>), reverberation <note>a chemical process involving intense, reflected heat</note>, and alkalization. These processes breed grievous diseases such as ulcers, scabs, tetters <note>a general term for various skin eruptions or rashes</note>, ringworms, itch, and the like, all of which are diseases arising from dissolved salt. 

Overindulgence <note>original: "Surfetting"</note> and gluttony, which destroy digestion, are the causes of the salt's destruction. Excess and luxury dissolve and melt the salt of man. The stars also push the salt out of its proper degree. This salt may be changed and turned into all sorts of salts, and the nature of the disease corresponds to the nature of that transmutation <note>transformation or change from one substance to another</note>. 

He says, therefore, that the sulphur kindled in a man's body is to be quenched with the sulphur of the "great world" <note>the macrocosm or external universe</note>, which harmonically agrees with the other. As was seen in the *New City* at *Prague* in the year *1602*, in a certain rustic or countryman of *Bohemia* named *Mathem*, who was about 36 years of age. The smallest of them was about the size of a pigeon's egg, and all of them together filled nearly four of my handfuls. By this bold and dangerous venture, and without damaging his health, he traveled about earning his living for many years together, etc.

## CHAPTER 5.

### *The Duty of Nature's Minister, the Physician.*

All common philosophy was not restricted to *Aristotle*, as *Petrus Ramus* <note>original: "P. Ramus"; a 16th-century French humanist and logician known for his rigorous critiques of Aristotelian logic.</note> has soundly <note>original: "foundly"; likely an OCR error for the Early Modern "soundly," where 's' and 'f' were often confused.</note> proved; nor was the entire *Light of Nature* <note>The concept of innate human reason and the direct observation of the natural world, as opposed to relying solely on scripture or ancient texts.</note> drawn into *Galen* and limited to him alone, as *Paracelsus* <note>A Swiss physician and alchemist who pioneered the use of chemicals and minerals in medicine, famously challenging the traditional "Galenic" medical system.</note> witnesses. No one ought to deprive another of the liberty of human ingenuity—that Light of Nature, the power to discern and judge as well as himself. The *Greek* monarchy <note>A metaphor meaning the absolute intellectual authority of ancient Greek thinkers like Aristotle and Galen over the sciences.</note> is at an end. 

Therefore, he who wishes to be an excellent physician must be free from every kind of sect (for no man can be said to be truly and thoroughly learned who is bound only to the basics of any one faculty alone). He must not be tied to the opinion of any one author, but should follow the naked truth and subscribe to it alone, always remembering the words of *Horace* <note>The Roman poet Horace famously wrote "Nullius addictus iurare in verba magistri," meaning "not bound to swear allegiance to any master," a motto often used by scientific reformers.</note>.

<summary>This page argues that medical knowledge is not the exclusive domain of ancient authorities like Aristotle or Galen, advocating instead for the "Light of Nature" and intellectual independence for physicians.</summary>
<keywords>medical philosophy, Aristotle, Galen, Paracelsus, Petrus Ramus, Horace, intellectual liberty, Light of Nature, medical sects</keywords>

### *I think and judge as I find cause:*
### *My rule is not another person's mind.*

<margin>Wherever the storm carries me, I am driven as a guest: I am not bound to swear allegiance to the words of any master. <note>original Latin: "Quo me cunque rapit tempestas, deferor hospes: Nullius addictus jurare in verba magistri." This is a famous quote from the Roman poet Horace, emphasizing intellectual independence.</note></margin>
It is not that other men’s inventions are to be completely disregarded by sticking only to one school of thought <term>original: "sect"</term>; for all schools, no matter how many there are, may well be accepted. This is because in every one of them there is something excellent that is not common to another, as said that most noble and wise Giovanni Pico della Mirandola <note>original: "Picus Mirandula" (1463–1494), a brilliant Italian Renaissance nobleman and philosopher.</note>, the "Phoenix of Philosophers" and the incomparable model of profound intelligence <term>original: "ingenuity"</term> and variety of learning.

There is no book so lowly and poor but has some good in it which the best authors have sometimes overlooked. This latter age, says Fabius <note>likely Marcus Fabius Quintilianus, the Roman rhetorician known as Quintilian.</note>, has endeavored to make the former age more complete; and because knowledge thrives as intelligence is improved, many loathsome errors of the ancient pagans <term>original: "Heathen"</term> have been wiped away (as if by a second song) by men of greater wisdom coming after them.

<margin>All secrets are destined to be discovered by divine decree.</margin>
Doubtless, there are more secrets still concealed in the treasures of wisdom and nature than we perceive. These—being ordained for specific times and nations by an unchangeable decree until the end of the world—are to be sought out by wise-hearted men.

For nature certainly, being cyclical <term>original: "Circularly," likely referring to the interconnected or recurring nature of knowledge and the physical world</term>, can hardly be fully understood by any mortal man because of the shortness of his life.

The case so standing, neither the **Medicine** <note>original: "Physick"</note> of the Ancients, nor that of *Theophrastus* <note>Theophrastus von Hohenheim, better known as Paracelsus (1493–1541), a Swiss physician and alchemist who challenged traditional medical authorities.</note>, is totally to be rejected. Nor yet should they be so embraced that if a better way is found, it should not also be received; for one day teaches another, and tomorrow may be master to today. For being but men, they have their failings. In some places they mistake; in others they write one thing contrary to another and thwart each other. Sometimes they differ from themselves; in many things they are deceived, nor does every man see all things. The Holy Spirit alone has the plenary <note>full or complete</note> knowledge of all things, distributing to every man according to his particular measure, blowing where it wills <note>original: "listeth"</note>, and reserving many things to himself so that we might always acknowledge him to be our only teacher.

A practitioner of spagyric medicine, which uses alchemical techniques like distillation and fermentation to extract the "essence" of plants or minerals.</note>, someone who can separate the pure from the impure and restore their patient to health through wise alchemical preparation. Truly, they should not be considered physicians who only make a great show of medicine <term>medicine; original: "Physick"</term> through verbal arguments and logic <term>logic; original: "Syllogistically"</term> but perform no cures at all. Some of them are so enraged against *Theophrastus* <note>Theophrastus von Hohenheim, better known as Paracelsus (1493–1541), a pioneer of the "medical revolution" who emphasized observation and chemistry over ancient authorities like Galen.</note>—because he opposed those proud and unrepentant men with all his might—that they would rather let their patients die than use his prescriptions. Many also despise his medicines for that reason: they fear that if they used them commonly and their wonderful effects became apparent, it would damage their own reputations. Therefore, the better the medicines are, the more these men undervalue them.

And although the critics of *Theophrastus*—those deceptive "Methodists" <note>In this context, "Methodists" refers to followers of the traditional Galenic "Method of Medicine," which focused on balancing humors, rather than the later religious movement.</note> who delay the cure and prolong the disease with their brief summaries—falsely charge genuine chemists <note>original: "Chymists"; practitioners of iatrochemistry or Paracelsian medicine which used minerals and chemicals.</note> with losing the right method, their accusation amounts to nothing. For it is plain to those whose understanding is enlightened that *Paracelsus* followed only the method that agreed with reason and experience. Nor should we be so devoted to any man's authority that we fail to prefer the truth above it; for without the truth, all authority is harmful <term>harmful; original: "pernicious"</term>, and all wise men will disregard it as being of no value compared with—

<summary>The author defends Paracelsus against traditional physicians who prioritize academic logic over successful cures, arguing that truth and experience should outweigh ancient medical authority.</summary>

out that: we should not care so much about who says it, as what is actually being said. No one should be so tied to another person’s judgment that they are completely deprived of their own.

True method consists of the knowledge of the disease and its cure—namely, which foods and appropriate medicines drive away sickness and bring about health. Therefore, *Wimpinaeus* <note>original: Wimpeneus; likely Johannes Wimpinaeus, a 16th-century physician and defender of Paracelsus</note> learnedly shows that the greatest and most serious diseases nowadays are cured in three ways by the *Paracelsian* <note>A follower of Paracelsus, a Swiss physician who pioneered the use of chemicals and minerals in medicine rather than traditional herbal "humors."</note> method.

First, because diseases are more perfectly understood. Previously, when all ailments were reduced to the four humors <note>The traditional Greek medical theory that health was governed by the balance of blood, phlegm, black bile, and yellow bile</note>, those that arose from *Tartar* <term>Tartar</term> <note>In Paracelsian medicine, "Tartar" refers to stony or gravel-like metabolic waste that calcifies or "cleaves" to the internal organs, similar to the scale found in wine casks or plaque on teeth.</note> clinging to any part of the body could not be cured, because it cannot be classified as one of those four humors. And since we have come upon the topic of *Tartar*, I will speak a little more about it.

<margin>Every body part has its own proper digestion, separation, waste-organ, and excrement within itself. The first digestion of the stomach is not full digestion, but a preparation for the digestions of the individual organs.</margin>
The first *Ens* <note>Latin: "Being" or essence</note> or "Being toward life," from which the body receives its nourishment and food, arises out of the final substance of the food through the *Archaeus* <term>Archaeus</term> <note>The "internal chemist" or vital force that Paracelsians believed directed the functions of the body and the digestion of food.</note>, or the digestion of the stomach, its separation of nutrients, and the generation of that separation. It is reduced to *Sulphur*, *Mercury*, and *Salt* <note>The "Three Primes" (Tria Prima) of Paracelsian alchemy, believed to be the basic components of all material things</note>, as is clear in the three chief <term>Emunctories</term> <note>Organs or ducts that carry off waste, such as the skin, kidneys, or bowels.</note>: the excess of *Salt* is separated by the urine; that of *Sulphur* is divided and purged through the bowels; and *Mercury*, or the moisture of what nourishes, passes through the pores by sweat if there is too much of it.

Whatever we eat and drink contains a mucous-like, clayish, and sandy *Tartar* that is harmful...

to our health. Nature takes nothing but that which is pure. The stomach—which is the instrument of man's <term>Archeus</term> <note>original: Archæus; a Paracelsian term for the vital force or "internal chemist" believed to govern the body's functions</note>, or the internal, innate <term>chemist</term> <note>original: Chymist</note> which God has planted in man—as soon as it takes anything into itself, it separates that which is impure, dreggy, and <term>tartarous</term> <note>original: Tartarous; referring to "tartar," the stony or waste-like deposits early medicine believed were left behind by food and drink</note> from the pure nourishment. If the stomach is strong in its full separative force, the pure parts pass to the members of the body for nourishment, and the impure goes out by succession <note>original: successe; the natural process of elimination</note>. If the stomach is weak and feeble, the impure matter is also sent through the <term>mesaraic</term> <note>original: Mesaraick; now known as the mesenteric veins, which carry blood from the digestive organs to the liver</note> veins to the liver, and there it is separated and digested a second time. The liver, therefore, in its course and in turn, separates the pure from the impure—<term>namely</term> <note>original: viz.</note>, the "Ruby" from the "Crystal." The Ruby is the nourishment of all the members, such as the heart, brain, and so on.

<margin>
The first separation of tartar, which occurs in the liver, turns into "moss"; the second, which occurs in the stomach, turns into "hail"; the third, in the kidneys and bladder, turns into a little stone. Every person has "moss" and "hail" within them, but not everyone has a stone.
</margin>

The <term>crystal</term> <note>original: Christall</note>, or that which is not nourishment, it sends to the <term>reins</term> <note>original: reines; the kidneys</note>. The liver resolves this into water and then throws it out. If the liver is weak and cannot separate well, it sends this matter to the kidneys in a mucilaginous and stony state. There, for want of good separation (that is, when the "spirits of salt"—namely, of the flesh and of the urine—are united), the spirit of the salt intervenes by the power of <term>predestination</term> <note>original: predestination; here meaning the predetermined or natural destiny of the substance to follow a specific path</note>, and it is coagulated into sand or <term>tartar</term>, which is either cloddy or slimy.

<term>Tartar</term>, therefore, is the waste from meat and drink, which is hardened by the spirit of salt.

<margin>Paracelsus says that the matter—namely, the Tartar of diseases—is twofold: bolous <note>lumpy or solid</note>, such as in dairy, fish, and meat; and viscous, bituminous <note>sticky like tar</note>, and mucilaginous, such as the waste from legumes, cabbage, roots, fruits, etc.</margin>

The first sort arises from the use of those things which grow in the Earth, such as legumes <note>original: "Pulse"</note> of all sorts, herbs, etc., which we eat.

The second comes from the nourishment which we receive from the Water, such as fish and shellfish.

The third is in the flesh of beasts and birds...

I do, I say, persuade them that they should forsake their fathers' faults and the errors of other men, and be warned against their ill-resolved purposes. His works, which were printed for the public good through the encouragement and at the cost of that most renowned and reverend *Ernestus*, Prince Elector of *Cologne*, have given occasion to his adversaries to charge him with implicit and evident contradiction. This is because he wrote in a <term>Magical style</term> <note>original: "Magicall ſtile"; a cryptic, symbolic way of writing intended to hide secrets from those not initiated into the craft.</note> for the unskillful and unlearned. He wrote not for the common sort, but for himself and the understanding children of <term>Wisdom</term> trained up in the School of <term>Magic</term>. He did not write for the <term>Sophistical Alchemists</term> <note>Alchemists who used false reasoning or deceptive practices.</note>, such as those who are bewitched with a greedy desire for gold. He was also incensed with a righteous hatred against some of the most wretched, wicked, and inhumane <term>physicians</term> and <term>druggists</term> of his time, who often lay in wait to poison him to shorten his life. If he had written more plainly, then those itinerant <note>original: "circumforaneous"; referring to those who wandered from market to market selling fraudulent goods or medical advice.</note> and vulgar <term>alchemists</term> would have surpassed all other <term>physicians</term> and prostituted the art to the great wrong and injury of Nature.

He concealed his mysteries under common and various names; therefore, we must not take the metaphors for the truth itself, or for that which is intended by them. For there are few who understand the <term>Physical Secrets</term>—that is, the hidden power of *God*—or the <term>Magical WORDS</term> in *Paracelsus*. Therefore, they need and require a <term>Delian swimmer</term> <note>A classical proverb referring to someone who can dive deep into difficult or obscure texts, famously used by Socrates to describe the philosophy of Heraclitus.</note>, a most acute and sharp wit, and a <term>Magical Understanding</term>—even that purified eye of the mind which can pry into and search out their sentences and secret mysteries. By <term>Magic</term> everywhere, I mean that which is *True*

<meta>This page continues a discussion on the nature of "lawful" magic or natural philosophy, contrasting it with forbidden sorcery, and proceeds to argue for the unification of internal medicine and surgery, as advocated by Paracelsus.</meta>

...and lawful—not the infamous and profane <unclear>goëtia</unclear> <note>original: "goëtia"; a Greek-derived term for sorcery or black magic</note> or witchcraft, which is fit for none but *Vulcan* <note>the Roman god of fire and metalworking; here likely referring to those who use fire for destructive or base purposes</note> and deserves to be burned. This dark magic is pursued by men of wretched wit who are obsessed with the most abominable curiosity. But this [lawful art] is the absolute perfection of the entire, most noble Philosophy, the highest wisdom of God's works, and the complete knowledge of hidden <note>original: "occult"</note> Nature. This knowledge commonly works incredible wonders and performs things exceeding the usual order of Nature through a fit application of "Agents" and "Patients" <note>In early science, "Agents" are active forces and "Patients" are the passive materials they act upon</note>, while observing how things agree or disagree with one another.

The intelligent readers will take notice that *Paracelsus* <note>Philippus Aureolus Theophrastus Bombastus von Hohenheim (1493–1541), a pioneering Swiss physician and alchemist</note> has joined the practice of both types of medicine together—meaning both internal medicine <note>original: "Physick"</note> and surgery <note>original: "Chyrurgie"</note>—imitating the example of the great *Hippocrates*. For he defined a twofold medicine: "Physical," or the knowledge of all diseases; and "Surgical," or the curing of the same, which, like a carpenter's work, cannot be done without hands. Neither can safely exist or be practiced without the other except with great hurt and danger to the sick. Therefore, it is necessary that every surgeon should be a physician, and every physician a surgeon, so that there may be a sound <note>original: "found"; likely a typographical error for "sound," meaning healthy or firm</note> bridegroom for a sound bride. The choice of medicines must also always be considered, and their preparations and compositions should be made by the physician himself, rather than carelessly left to others. He is truly a genuine physician who knows how (not only by reason, as mean...

<meta>The text continues a discussion on the duties of a "rational physician" in preparing their own remedies rather than relying on others.</meta>

Rational physicians do so, by preparing with their own hands the medicines <note>original: "medicaments"</note> which they know, in order to separate and purge them from their poisons and dregs, to reduce them to their pure simplicity, and not leave them to an unskillful cook. For the bad and good are mixed together; neither is sugar free from foul <term>impurity</term> <note>original: "fæculent impurity"; referring to the sediment or dregs of a substance.</note>, nor is honey void of a venomous bitterness. When the medicines are prepared, the physician must—with true faith and accurate skill or judgment—apply them to human necessities so that the root of the disease may be plucked up; by this means, his honest and quick efficiency <note>original: "dispatch"</note> will prevent the patient's complaints.

A physician, therefore, should have both the theory and the practice; he must both know and prepare his medicines, for judgment without practice is altogether barren and fruitless. Medicine <note>original: "Physick"</note> is learned by the labor of the hands and practical work or manufacturing, as the fire <note>A reference to the "fire" of the laboratory or furnace used in chemical and alchemical distillation.</note> discovers new and most pleasant remedies continually, which Nature daily more and more purges from their excesses and then delivers to her servant to prepare. But the *great doctors* of our time, who have grown gray in the common <note>original: "vulgar"</note> art of medicine, will not become scholars and schoolboys again; *to dig they are ashamed* <note>A biblical allusion to the Parable of the Unjust Steward in Luke 16:3, suggesting these doctors are too proud to perform the manual labor of pharmacy.</note>. And as it is hard to move an old tree, or to bring an old dog to the collar and make him a good hunter, so these men will openly contradict the truth. With an obstinate, dog-like howling, they bark at it and bite it rather than endeavor to reform their errors.

<summary>This page argues that a true physician must personally prepare and purify their own medicines through practical laboratory work rather than relying on unskillful others. <margin>We should always be employed for the good of our neighbor and avoid idleness because it is the Devil’s cushion, the mother of vice, and the stepmother of virtue. To prevent this mischief in the future and to remove the stigma from <term>physicians</term>—caused by the public outcry against their uncertain art—it would be wisely done and worth their labor if those who study <term>medicine</term> would take action. They prefer labor and sweat (for which God is committed to bestow all things) over the idleness and sloth which most men so much desire.

<meta>This page continues a discussion on the spiritual and psychological roots of health and disease, specifically focusing on the relationship between medicine, faith, and the human imagination.</meta>

<margin>The physician and the medicine <term>original: "Physick"</term> are the true mercy of God.</margin>

He who despises His Word despises medicine, and vice versa; for he who says medicine is worth nothing essentially claims that there is no God.

<margin>A cheerful heart is a constant feast.</margin>

<margin>The physician who is most highly regarded does the most good.</margin>
the sick in their physician and their medicine. Natural faith—I am not speaking of the "faith of grace" through Christ, but the faith born of God the Father during the first Creation, which is our IMAGINATION—is so powerful that it can both cause and cure diseases. This is plain during times of plague <note>original: "Pestilence"</note>. When a person's own imagination, driven by fear and terror, gives birth to that "Basilisk of Heaven" <note>The "Basilisk of Heaven" refers to a poisonous celestial influence or a lethal internal corruption triggered by intense fear.</note>, it intoxicates the inner universe <term>original: "microcosmicall Firmament"</term> of the individual through their own superstition, just as the patient’s faith assists in the process. <margin>The imagination is like pitch; it sticks easily and catches fire quickly.</margin>

<margin>Therefore, says <term>Damascen</term> <note>Likely St. John of Damascus (c. 675–749), an influential theologian whose works touched on the relationship between the body and soul</note>: It is necessary to imprint and promise health to the sick, and not to abandon hope even if the patient despairs.</margin>

He who falls mad from the bite of a mad dog will have the shape of dogs appearing in his urine. Likewise, the lust and longing of a pregnant woman works upon another body when, through forgetting herself, she imprints the mark of the thing she longed for upon the child in her womb, as <note>The text continues on the next page regarding the effects of maternal cravings, such as "saffron" marks.</note>

<meta>This page contains a modern introductory disclaimer and usage guide provided by Google for digitized public domain books. Although the text is contemporary, it is formatted here to align with the modernization standards for the collection.</meta>

<lang>English</lang>

This is a digital copy of a book that was preserved for generations on library shelves before it was carefully scanned by Google as part of a project to make the world's books discoverable online.

It has survived long enough for the copyright to expire and for the book to enter the public domain. A public domain book is one that was never subject to copyright or whose legal copyright term has expired. Whether a book is in the public domain may vary from country to country. Public domain books are our gateways to the past, representing a wealth of history, culture, and knowledge that is often difficult to discover.

Marks, notations, and other marginal notes <note>original: "marginalia"; refers to handwritten notes or marks made in the margins of a book</note> present in the original volume will appear in this file—a reminder of this book's long journey from the publisher to a library and finally to you.

### Usage guidelines

Google is proud to partner with libraries to digitize public domain materials and make them widely accessible. Public domain books belong to the public and we are merely their custodians. Nevertheless, this work is expensive, so in order to keep providing this resource, we have taken steps to prevent abuse by commercial parties, including placing technical restrictions on automated searching <note>original: "querying"</note>.

We also ask that you:

+ **Make non-commercial use of the files** We designed Google Book Search for use by individuals, and we request that you use these files for personal, non-commercial purposes.

+ **Refrain from automated searching** Do not send automated searches of any sort to Google's system. If you are conducting research on machine translation, optical character recognition <note>OCR: the technology used to convert images of printed text into editable, searchable digital text</note>, or other areas where access to a large amount of text is helpful, please contact us. We encourage the use of public domain materials for these purposes and may be able to help.

+ **Maintain attribution** The Google “watermark” you see on each file is essential for informing people about this project and helping them find additional materials through Google Book Search. Please do not remove it.

+ **Keep it legal** Whatever your use, remember that you are responsible for ensuring that what you are doing is legal. Do not assume that just because we believe a book is in the public domain for users in the United States, that the work is also in the public domain for users in other countries. Whether a book is still in copyright varies from country to country, and we cannot offer guidance on whether any specific use of any specific book is allowed. Please do not assume that a book's appearance in Google Book Search means it can be used in any manner anywhere in the world. Copyright infringement liability can be quite severe.

### About Google Book Search

Google's mission is to organize the world's information and to make it universally accessible and useful. Google Book Search helps readers discover the world's books while helping authors and publishers reach new audiences. <meta>This page continues the discussion of the "Fifth Essence," specifically comparing the life-giving "supernal fire" found in celestial bodies to the destructive "elemental fire" found on Earth. It argues that medicine must be derived from the former to truly preserve the body.</meta>

->[ 209 ]<-

<margin>The Celestial Spirit, or that Spirit of the world, and the Natural Spirit of our body is one and the same Spirit; and therefore the Heat of the Sun and Moon, generated from the very pulse of the spirit, is a thing more **concoct** <term>concoct</term> <note>original: "concoct"; meaning more "cooked" or refined by natural heat, signifying a higher state of maturity or perfection.</note> and consequently more perfect.</margin>
...or is less restrained by matter. For these things work more quickly and perfectly, and do more readily beget their like; and, what is easier, the spirit or that **supernal Fire** <term>supernal Fire</term> <note>A divine or heavenly fire associated with the stars and the sun, believed to be the source of life and growth.</note> is extracted from them by art. To this spirit, the heat that burns not like the **Elemental**, but that which makes all things fruitful, and Light giving life to all things, are proper. But burning heat, consuming all things, and darkness, making all things barren, are proper to the **Elemental and Inferior Fire**.

That same burning heat is therefore excluded, as are all diverse and contrary things, such as all the inferior elements. For this and all things else that include a natural composition in them (so far as they are yet drowned in a thick, gross matter, and as yet not separated from it) are subject to **corruption** <note>Decay or the natural breakdown of physical matter.</note> and **transmutation** <term>transmutation</term> <note>The process of changing from one state or substance into another; here implying instability.</note>. But **Medicines** ought to be preserving and very durable, and remote from corruption. For whereas they should preserve the human body from corruption, they ought first to be of a long and lasting nature; otherwise they would corrupt rather than preserve.

<margin>One Like added to its like maketh it more Like.</margin>
Besides, it is but in vain to think to preserve a corruptible body by a putrid and corruptible thing, to cure the weak by a feeble thing, or to form a thing by a thing subject to deformity. Every corruptible, weak, and feeble thing added to its like increases it, and so that corruptibility is increased, not diminished—as we see in some (and truly too many) **Physicians** of our time who labor in vain to cure men of their maladies...

->O 4<-
<meta>catchword: by</meta>

...fashion, do not reject good meat because of the cook's lack of skill. Indeed, the princely delicacies of this royal banquet deserve a more refined hand to present them to the world. For the sake of proper manners, I have for a long time excused myself from this service, *so that it might be given to one more worthy* <note>original Latin: *ut detur digniori*</note>, preferring and offering it to those with greater abilities who were more fit to have undertaken it. Had they accepted, these incomparable mysteries and unsearchable truths would have been more *alchemically* <note>original: *Chymically*; likely referring to the alchemical process of purifying or distilling a substance to its essence</note> prepared, rather than offered to you in the crude form of such a dull language. I have only this for my *justification* <note>original: *Apology*</note>: that I have been more careful to be *faithful* than *ornate* <note>original: *curious*</note>, striving not so much to decorate the garment as to make it fit the body. If others had shown their skill, I would not be judged as a bungler <term>bungler: an unskilled or clumsy worker</term>.

But, reader, I am not as focused on making my own excuses as I am on not leaving you without a *warning*: therefore, I advise you as that most profound *Germanic Philosopher* <note>original: *Teutonick Philosopher*</note> **Jacob Boehme** <note>original: **Jacob Behmen** (1575–1624), a famous German Christian mystic and theologian</note> says somewhere in his writings: *If your mind is not spiritual, refrain from reading these things, for they will do you more harm than good. Immanuel <note>original: "Emanuel"; a Hebrew name meaning "God with us"</note>; if you do not know the extent of the Word made flesh <note>original: "Incarnate Word"</note>; if you do not understand the *angelic ascent and descent* on *Jacob’s ladder*, how can you add your voice <note>original: "cast thy suffrage"</note> to that *seraphic praise* <note>seraphic: relating to the seraphim, the highest order of angels</note>, **Holy, Holy, Holy is the Lord of hosts,** Isaiah 6:3, **the whole earth is full of his glory?**

<meta>This page continues the author's reflections on his past actions and writings; the catchword from the previous page was "learned."</meta>

<margin>In *Nil Novi* <note>Latin: "Nothing New"; likely a reference to a previous work by the author.</note>.</margin>
Two things afflict me deeply, and for the third, I am profoundly grieved.

I am sorry that I ever yielded to any provocation and prepared myself to fight in my own (yet not entirely my own) quarrel, as I did against Parson Gosse and others. Their harmful and unjust actions might have been justly described more severely by another person's pen; however, it was not as important for me to seek revenge as it was to endure the wrong. I might lessen this fault to a very small guilt, having taken the old <term>Protestation</term> <note>The Protestation of 1641 was an oath intended to defend the Protestant religion, the King, and the liberties of the subject.</note> to preserve and defend the liberty of the people and seek the Reformation of the Church—both of which have been so greatly violated and obstructed by men motivated by worldly interests. For the future, I trust I will conduct myself as one who commits his way to the Lord with patience and good deeds, bearing the Cross daily and descending into death through cheerful obedience and self-denial. In this way, I may "fill up the sufferings of Christ" <note>A reference to the biblical verse Colossians 1:24, suggesting that the author’s own hardships contribute to the ongoing spiritual work of Christ.</note> that remain in my mortal body.

<margin>2 Corinthians 12:2</margin>
I have learned by painful experience that I should have hidden that pearl until I had sold everything (Matthew 13:44) and pondered those unutterable words longer in my heart, so that they might come forth with more maturity. Fourteen years would have been little time enough to have ripened and prepared such a deep and inward vision for public participation. The harm <note>original: "prejudice," meaning injury or detriment</note> of this rashness has been mostly to myself; leave me alone, and do not try to comfort me until I have bitterly mourned it. --- Page 254 ---
# Postscript.

## Reader,

**W**hile I have proposed and emphasized the creation of God as an object for your admiration and a motive for his adoration <note>original: "adoration" refers to worship and praise</note>, it is not in the least intended to diminish the due reverence rightfully belonging to that even richer mercy of the Lord granted to humanity: the giving and preserving of those sacred records of heavenly mysteries contained in the most holy Scriptures. My desire instead is that both of those "Books of God"—*Nature* and *Scripture* <note>The "Two Books" was a common 17th-century metaphor suggesting that God reveals himself through both the natural world (the Book of Nature) and the Bible (the Book of Scripture).</note>—might be better studied and more carefully observed. Do I seek to make the Word of the Lord void through his works? God forbid! On the contrary, I establish the one by the other.

---

<summary>In this postscript, the author clarifies that his focus on the natural world as a means of praising God is intended to complement, rather than replace, the study of the Bible.</summary>
<keywords>Creation, Scripture, Nature, Divine Revelation, Theology, Two Books of God</keywords>

# *Added by a Friend of the Publisher of this work by Crollius and Paracelsus in English.*

---

## **CROLLIUS.**

Two things are seen within this volume small:
*the origins of the great and the lesser worlds* <note>original: "the great and the lesse Worlds Originall"; referring to the Macrocosm (the universe) and the Microcosm (man)</note>.
Here a man may see his own shape as if in a mirror,
*by which he may completely escape corruption.*
The author who wrote this was divinely taught,
*and so likewise was the person who translated it.*
Here may be seen what nature is and what grace is,
*what God's "back parts" are, and what His "face" is* <note>A biblical allusion to Exodus 33:23, distinguishing between God’s hidden essence and His visible works in nature</note>.
Here heaven and earth are both in harmony,
*a cure to ease us of our vanity.*
The true Elixir is here, the stone <term>The Philosopher's Stone</term> that
*transforms <note>original: "transmute"</note> both the outward and the inward man:*
And makes all things heavenly, like fine crystal,
*yes, like Christ, the divine prototype.*
What is above is likewise here below <note>The central Hermetic maxim "As above, so below," suggesting the physical world reflects the spiritual world</note>,
*as this anatomy of man shows.*
Man, in all his parts, consists
*of that which the Macrocosm <term>the great world; the universe</term> is composed.*
The world itself is a man, though great and big,
*and man himself is a world; only dig here,*
A treasure will appear worth more than all,
*by which he may be ransomed from his fall.*

<summary>This introductory poem praises the work of Oswald Crollius, framing it as a divine guide to understanding the Hermetic relationship between the universe (Macrocosm) and humanity (Microcosm). It suggests that by studying this "anatomy of man," a reader can achieve spiritual transformation and "escape corruption."</summary>
<keywords>Oswald Crollius, Paracelsus, alchemy, Macrocosm, Microcosm, Philosopher's Stone, Hermeticism, spiritual transformation, anatomy of man</keywords>

Consequently, many things, after the passing of a *Platonic year* <note>original: "Platonick year"; traditionally a cycle of about 25,800 years where the stars return to their original positions, here used metaphorically to mean a long period of refinement or a full cycle of study</note>, have been returned for free and with interest to their owners—whole, entire, and far more complete—even though I originally acquired them in a ragged and broken state at a high price. I was often enticed by rumors of hidden secrets to travel to visit various people at great difficulty and expense, only to find that meeting them or seeing their work in person greatly diminished my opinion of them, as often happens in such cases. The things they bragged about as great secrets seemed to me either common or so faulty and defective that I had to correct the flaws in their many descriptions. I reduced these into a single method, which I first proved through my own *chemical* <note>original: "Chymicall"</note> trials. Hence it comes to pass that without Nature’s *Vulcan* <note>original: Vulcan; the Roman god of fire, here symbolizing the transformative power of heat in alchemical processes</note>, whom the poets praise as the truest inventor and teacher of the mysteries of the arts, the greatest part of those (I say this without intending offense to those great men) who have written in our time about the more secret spagyrical <term>alchemical; specifically the process of separating and recombining the "essential" parts of a substance</term> preparations have done so based on the reports of others rather than their own hands-on experience. <meta>This page continues the author’s advice on the preparation of medicines and begins a defense of his decision to publish these secrets.</meta>

...their sick neighbors, of whatever rank and condition, who in the fear of God shall also use these medicines. These medicines must be rightly prepared by one’s own labor, using a proper and <term>artificial</term><note>original: "artificiall"; in this context, it means "skillfully made" or "technical," rather than "fake" or "unnatural."</note> method. One must not trust (unless they wish to be unsuccessful) the deceptive and fraudulent preparations of others, as many people do. By doing so, they will experience through their own practice far greater efficacy and results in the cures they perform than I can or ought to describe—especially if the medicines are administered according to the nature of the patient and with the approval of a physician present. All of this, by God's help, will be made manifest by "Truth, the daughter of Time." <note>original: "Truth the daughter of time" (Veritas filia temporis); a common Renaissance proverb suggesting that the truth of a matter is eventually revealed as time passes.</note>

<margin>
Ignorance is the inseparable companion of pride and mischief.
</margin>

But what danger do I now expose myself to by this public service? What, I ask, should be done amidst such a diversity and hazard of opinions? What shall a man do in the middle of so many carping critics, who cast the worst judgments on the best things? <margin>Worldly-minded physicians do nothing but for profit and praise; whereas the goal of medicine is not to scrape up money, but to discover the secrets of Nature and show the physician's love to the sick.</margin>
...with a multitude of frivolous services and sugared words to cloak their simple-mindedness, sneaking away and evading the disease, while discouraging the patient with the difficulty of the first and most important treatments. This is how the common sort of proud, conceited <term>physicians</term> <note>original: "Phisitians"</note> act, ingratiating themselves with the wealthy for base profit, while completely ignoring others of humbler means.

&emsp;&emsp;Moreover, such is the baseness and malice of some among this <term>rabble</term> <note>original: "ribble rabble," meaning a disorganized or contemptible group</note> that with arrogant words they vilify, slight, condemn, and everywhere forbid—labeling them as poisonous—those medical secrets and inventions of <term>chemists</term> <note>original: "Chymists," referring to early practitioners of alchemical medicine</note> (whom they call "charcoal-burner-like" physicians <note>original: "Collier-like-Phisitians"; "Collier" was a derogatory term for those who worked with furnaces and fire</note>). Meanwhile, they claim for themselves the praise due to the true author, robbing the inventors of the art and their benefactors of their deserved honor through lying and deceitful theft, so that they might more elegantly and with greater display use those medicines which they obtained with such craftiness. To these <term>boastful pretenders</term> <note>original: "Apulejan Bravadoes," a reference to Apuleius, author of "The Golden Ass," suggesting someone masquerading in a skin not their own</note>—whether they wear the skin of a lion or a fox—admission to the "Bath of *Diana*" <note>A metaphor for the purity of nature’s secrets or the alchemical process</note> should not be granted. This is because *Pythagoras* forbids putting our food into a <term>chamber pot</term> <note>original: "close stool"</note>; nor should we bestow our choicest herbs on those who rudely rush into the <term>chemist's</term> garden, since brambles and thistles will serve their purposes well enough. But since the slanders and reproaches of wicked and ignorant men are, in the opinion of the wise, not to be regarded by...

...heroic spirits; and just as the fly never falls into the boiling pot, the more humane and civil inclinations of some also lead me to expect better things. Nor would I, because of the unworthiness of some, shut these doors against those who seek the true and ancient medicine <note>original: "Physick"</note>, who are seriously considering and daily working out its ancient knowledge, opportunely forsaking their errors and giving place—without envy or evil speech—to *Paracelsus* <note>Theophrastus von Hohenheim (1493–1541), a physician and alchemist who pioneered the use of chemicals and minerals in medicine.</note> according to his merit in practice and preparations.

Neither will this discourse offend any holy and learned physician; I intend it only against those proud, envious, and ignorant servants <note>original: "vassalls"</note> who oppose chemical truth <note>original: "Chymicall verity"</note> against their own consciences, to the reproach of God and Nature, and to the hindrance of the common good.

## CHAPTER I.

# Of True Medicine.

<margin>1</margin>
<margin>See Paracelsus's *Labyrinth of Physicians*.</margin>
<margin><insert>Possibly Platonic principles—plenitude or goodness</insert></margin>
<margin>The master teaches that student in vain whose birth <note>original: "Nativity"; here referring to an astrological birth chart</note> is not disposed to that art by the stars.</margin>

**T**he true medicine <term>Physick: Historically, the art of healing or the study of natural philosophy.</term> which I intend to treat here by divine assistance is the pure gift of the most high God. It is not to be sought for or learned from the heathens, but from God alone—the "Ancient of Days" and the "Father of Lights"—who cannot err, the one and only Governor of the supreme universe. Wisdom, therefore, is not to be obtained from created things <note>original: "the Creatures"</note>, but from God. Since he is the first inventor of all secrets, he alone knows what properties he has endowed in every creature. Therefore, no mortal master or "dead letter" <note>Written texts lacking the living spirit of the author.</note> can ever teach it as well as he who is the perfect artist of all things: even the most high Creator and glorious God. From him, it flows to us like heat from the sun's beams, which produces all kinds of flowers and herbs; for what does man have that he has not received from above? All learn from the first <note>Likely referring to the "First Man" (Adam), who was believed to have perfect knowledge of nature before the Fall.</note> by regression (or going backward), and this "first" learned from God, who gave him knowledge at his creation. A physician should be born out of the light of grace and the nature of the inward and invisible man—the internal angel, or "light of nature" <term>Light of Nature: A central concept in Paracelsian philosophy referring to the divine wisdom inherent in the natural world and the human soul.</term>—which, like a reliable doctor, teaches and instructs men just as the Holy Spirit taught

<margin>Medicine <note>original: "Physick"</note> is a favor given by God. University books are not its foundation, but rather the invisible mercy of God and His special gift; this is also true of those writings that depend upon the true foundation and experience. This medicinal essence in medicine is called Gold.</margin>

Therefore, medicine is nothing else but the created and incarnate mercy of our Heavenly Father, bestowed upon poor afflicted mortals. This is so that the sick patient might sensibly perceive and experience the bountiful love, mercy, and assistance of his Creator during his afflictions, so that God may be glorified in all His wonderful works.

Now this medicine—the natural "mummy" <note>original: "Mummy"; here referring to a vital curative substance or life-force believed to exist in living things</note> and kernel of Nature—is contained in the vital sulphur <note>one of the three primary principles in Paracelsian alchemy, representing the soul or the principle of combustibility and life</note> as if in the treasure-house of Nature. It is founded in the balsam <note>a preservative essence or natural healing agent</note> of vegetables, minerals, and animals, from which every action in Nature begins. By its power alone all diseases are cured, provided that (as shall be shown shortly) it is rightly prepared, separated from all impurity, and administered in proper order by a godly, skillful physician to the poor, weak, and decayed nature of man.

Together, philosophy and astronomy create an internal and perfect philosopher, not only in the "great world" <note>the universe or macrocosm</note> but also in the "lesser world" <note>humankind or the microcosm</note>. Therefore, it is necessary to adapt the disposition of the great world as a parent to the little world as a son, and to duly compare the anatomy of the world with the anatomy of man.

The outward world is a theoretical anatomy where we may see, as if in a mirror, the lesser world of Man. Much of his wonderful and excellent fabric and creation is necessary for a physician to know, yet it cannot be understood by looking at the man himself. This is because they do not agree in outward form or physical substance, but rather in all their powers and virtues. As the great world is, so is the lesser; in essence and internal form, they are altogether one and the same thing. It is only the outward form that distinguishes the world from man.

What the Light of Nature is.

Without the knowledge of the Light of Nature or the great world, no physician can have an exact knowledge of diseases in Man.

The great world is a reflection and mirror of the little world, Man.

A <term>physician</term> must spring out of Nature, for within him, from him, and of him there is nothing but Nature itself. Nature, not man, makes a <term>physician</term>. Because the physical matter of Man is the extract of the <term>four elements</term> <note>ancient and medieval science held that all matter was composed of earth, air, fire, and water</note>, it is necessary that he has within himself a familiarity with all the elements and their fruits, since he cannot live without them. food and **medicine** <note>original: "Physick"</note>. Therefore, all the external elements represent to us the whole man; when these are known, man is also understood, for they are alike and are the very **Microcosm** <note>The "small world"—the philosophical idea that the human body reflects the structure and components of the entire universe</note>. Within the four elements, there is but one **Anatomy**, essence, and matter; the only difference is in the **Form**. Thus, in all things, there is **Fire, Air, and Earthy Water**. So, there is a fourfold **man**; for **God** is far more wonderful in his invisible works than in his visible ones.

<margin>The knowledge of medicine in the outward world is to be drawn from the *Limbus*, and depends upon the knowledge of man.</margin> <note>original: "Limbovn"—referring to the *Limbus*, the primordial matter from which Paracelsus believed all things were created</note>

**Paracelsus** says that to avoid an **emptiness** in any of the four elements, God created living creatures that are "inanimate"—that is to say, without an **intellectual soul**. These are the four kinds of inhabitants of the elements, who differ from men (created after the **Image of God**) in their understanding, wisdom, arts, activities, and dwellings.

To the **Water** belong **Nymphs**, **Undines** <note>original: "Undens," water spirits</note>, and **Melusines** <note>original: "Melosyns," female spirits of fresh water</note>, whose **monsters** or hybrids are the **Sirens** that swim upon the water.

To the **Earth** belong **Gnomes**, **Lemurs**, **Sylphs**, **Mountain-dwellers** <note>original: "Montans"</note>, and **Zonnets** <note>spirits associated with the earth</note>, whose **monsters** are the **Pygmies**.

In the Air, or our airy world, there are shadowy spirits <note>original: Umbratils</note>, forest spirits <note>original: Silvestres</note>, and satyrs, whose monstrous offspring are the giants.

Thus, there is also a fourfold medicine. For example, the fiery, airy, watery, and earthy heart of the <term>Macrocosm</term> <note>the "Great World" or the universe as a whole</note> corresponds in all things to the heart of the <term>Microcosm</term> <note>the "Little World," specifically Man as a reflection of the universe</note>. All operations function the same way in man as they do in the universe. We are to understand the rest of the body's members in the same way; for the Microcosm (the child) must always correspond to the fourfold members of the Macrocosm (its parent). 

Thus, we find that every illness and its cure share the same outward appearance <term>Physiognomy</term>, palm-lines <term>Chiromancy</term>, and internal structure <term>Anatomy</term>. He who does not understand this fundamental principle cannot be a good physician. Thus, we also find from ancient records that astrologers and chemists <note>original: Chymiologists</note> were very closely related. Celestial astronomy is, in a sense, the parent and mistress of the "inferior" astronomy (chemistry). Both have their own heavens, their own suns, their own moons, their planets, and their own specific stars; however, the astrology of the higher realms interacts with the chemistry <note>original: Chymiology</note> of things here below. These three principles, which exist in all bodies, are entirely distinct in their use and properties because of the way their powers and operations are mixed, even though to our senses they appear to be a single, uniform substance.

<margin>The Spirit of God moved upon the face of the waters.</margin>

<meta>This page continues a discussion on the relationship between the four elements, specifically focusing on the roles of Air and Fire (the Firmament) in the creation and sustenance of the natural world.</meta>

<page-num>40</page-num>

<margin>Astors <note>original: "Astors"; likely a reference to an alchemical or philosophical authority</note> says that the Air was created before any other creature.</margin>
It happens and is ordered in such a way that the Water does not produce more than the Earth can sustain, the Air can nourish, and the Fire can consume.

<margin>All moist things drawn by the Sun from the Earth are consumed in the Air, whose fruits resemble Tereniabin <note>original: "Tereniabin"; a Persian term for a honey-like manna found on plants</note> or the richness of manna.</margin>
The Air is also twofold, for it has its own element as an inhabitant within itself. It is the restorative balsam <note>original: "Balsam"; in alchemy, a life-preserving essence or fluid</note> of all created things and the life of the other three elements. There is no element that God created more subtle or refined, which lives of its own accord and gives life to all things. Without it, neither the firmament, nor water, nor earth can produce their fruits. Fire cannot even burn without the air, much less can the "coals of heaven"—those growths of fire—shine.

<margin>The element of fire, according to Paracelsus <note>original: "Parac."; Philippus Aureolus Theophrastus Bombastus von Hohenheim (1493–1541), a famous Swiss physician and alchemist</note>, is the firmament of heaven.</margin>
The firmament, or fire, is likewise twofold and has its own element as an inhabitant within itself. This element contains all <term>astras</term> <note>original: "Astra's"; in Paracelsian philosophy, these are the star-like, spiritual seeds or celestial influences of things</note> and seeds. The element of fire, or the physical firmament, sends the bodies of the stars, sun, moon, and planets out from itself. Just as herbs, flowers, and trees grew out of the earth and yet remain in the earth, so at the Creation did the bodies of the stars grow out of heaven, yet they remain in the firmament (or heaven), swimming in their orbits as birds fly in the air.

In fit and certain seasons, these bring forth fruits as messengers of health or sickness. So it is that the <term>Three First</term> are the cause of all diseases; for in whatever body they are united, that body may be judged to be sound. Just as the loadstone <term>loadstone: a naturally magnetic rock.</term>, by drawing iron to itself, sucks out its spirit and leaves it rusty, so man has a twofold loadstone in respect to his body. On one hand, he draws the Astras <note>original: "Aſtra's"; celestial influences or powers emanating from the stars.</note> to himself, from which he sucks his food—such as worldly wisdom, sense, and thought—just as bees take honey from flowers and herbs. On the other hand, by his attractive power, he entices and lures the daily nourishment of his flesh and blood to himself from the Elements. And as the elemental body draws the elemental bodies to it by hunger and thirst, so the siderial spirit of Man attracts all arts, sciences, skills, and all human wisdom from the rays or beams of the superior stars or constellations. For the firmament is the Light of Nature <note>A Paracelsian concept meaning the innate, natural wisdom provided by God through the created world, distinct from spiritual revelation.</note>, which naturally supplies man with all things.

Hence it is plain that diseases are not cured by "contraries"—as if heat were used to expel cold, as though a person were to have the Elements themselves banished and driven out of them. Instead, they are cured by the secret things or *Astras* which the chemist <note>original: "Chymist," here referring to an alchemist</note> can reduce from the "last matter" <note>the finished physical product</note> back into the "first matter" <note>the pure, essential substance from which a thing originates</note>. These *Arcana* <note>meaning "hidden secrets" or "mysteries," referring to powerful alchemical medicines</note> are actually neither cold nor hot, yet they remove all diseases, just as an axe cuts down a tree despite the axe itself being neither cold nor hot. Of this sort are the "Fifth Essences" <note>the Quintessence, or the purest part of a substance</note>, "Magisteries" <note>concentrated alchemical preparations</note>, and the like.

<margin>He who knows himself knows God; for God will dwell nowhere but in man, in whom He is most clearly seen. We see God from within.</margin>
Because man has the true and real possession of all things and natures in himself, as well as the special and perfect Image even of the Creator of all things, the knowledge of all things and natures, and of the Creator himself (in which alone true Wisdom and Blessedness consist) must take its rise from the knowledge of oneself. So that man, when he rightly understands himself, may in himself—as in a kind of Divine mirror <note>original: Deified glass</note>—behold and understand all things. In this respect, *David* says in *Psalm 139:14: "I am fearfully and wonderfully made; marvelous are your works."*

On the contrary, he who does not know himself cannot have any true intrinsic and essential knowledge of things. Like a brute beast, whatever he knows outside of himself shall remain outside of him. For there is no knowledge—whether infused from Heaven or attained by the labor of industry and earthly diligence—that will remain in the soul forever; instead, it is subject to forgetfulness and will vanish. <meta>This page continues a theological or philosophical discussion on the source of spiritual wisdom. The catchword at the bottom "ly" indicates the next page begins with "lying" or "lyeth."</meta>

Spiritual knowledge does not come from human nature <note>original: "flesh and blood"</note>, nor from a vast number of books and reading, <margin>*Dionysius, On Divine Names* <note>original: "Dionys. lib. Divinors Nominum." Dionysius the Areopagite was a Christian theologian and philosopher.</note></margin> nor from a wealth of experience and old age. It is not found in the enticing words of human wisdom or the arguments of reason. Instead, the mind of man is perfected and completed by a passive reception of divine things—not through study and hard work, but through patience and submission.

The entire matter rests in knowledge, because we are made of everything and carry everything within us, just as God our Father does. The Son possesses all things equally with his Father <margin>*John 4 & 14*</margin>. Therefore, all natural and spiritual good things were and are in humanity from the beginning. However, just as that divine nature <note>original: "Character"</note> was darkened within us by sin, once sin is paid for and removed, that nature shines out again more and more. 

The understanding <note>original: "Notion"</note> of all things is created within us and with us; it is hidden in the very center of the Spirit. We only need to wake up from our slumbering and snoring <note>original: "snorting." In this period, "snorting" referred to the sound of heavy sleep or snoring.</note>. Because of sin, we have fallen asleep among the gifts that God has bestowed upon us, so that we can neither see, perceive, nor believe that these good things are currently within us. 

The understanding of man is capable of the highest learning and achievements. <margin>Pre-existence of the soul</margin> Or, according to *Plato*, it is full of all knowledge <note>original: "Sciences"</note> before it is joined to the body. While it is oppressed by the body, this knowledge lies hidden like fire buried in ashes; but once it is roused and stirred up from those physical influences <note>original: "humours"</note>, it shines forth and reveals those riches which previously lay hidden in their storehouses. Unless all the treasures of heaven—

He who believes in God by loving him clings to God; he who clings to God is one spirit with him.
</margin>

On the contrary, the perfect righteousness, true wisdom, and chief happiness of man is to know his Source <note>original: "Principle"</note>, God the Creator of all things, and to love him in all purity and uprightness. To this purpose our Savior says truly (*John 17:3*), *“This is eternal life: to know the Father, the true God, and Jesus Christ.”* That is <note>original: "viz." (videlicet)</note>, the believer should acknowledge and worship the Trinity, for this knowledge alone gives eternal life. For he who lives the life of Christ is born a son of God; he who is born a son of God must of necessity have the Father; he who has the Father is sealed with the Holy Spirit. He who knows the Son knows the Father also, for they are but one. 

The knowledge of God is blessedness and eternal life; for he who knows God in Christ is made the habitation and temple of God, and so is deified <note>term: Deified; in this context, to be made divine or godlike through spiritual union with God</note>, for he is a son of God born of God. And just as through the knowledge of the visible world we come to know the invisible Workman <note>Referring to God as the creator/architect of the universe</note>, so also from the visible Christ—or the life of Christ—we learn to know the Father, for he is the way to the Father. And as no one can come to the Son unless he hears and learns from the Father, so no one can rightly know the structure <note>original: "frame"</note> of the world except he who is taught by God.

Hence it is plain that what the heathens <note>Non-Christian ancient philosophers, such as the Greeks and Romans</note> wrote concerning nature is for the most part false, because their philosophy and other abilities were polluted and corrupt. It is in vain, therefore, to seek knowledge from those who have

Believers shall be taught by God when the Holy Spirit is poured forth.

To be brief, the knowledge of God is the treasury of the whole world in which all things are stored, so that without this knowledge no one can reach eternal life. Anyone who seeks for themselves what belongs to another inevitably falls into two troubles: the theft and robbery of oneself, and death.
