The Fall was a swerving from Unity to Alterity <note>Alterity refers to the state of being "other" or different, indicating a separation from the oneness of God.</note>.

Despising his Creator, he learned by experience what his own specific evil and *nothingness* were, to his own chosen damage and ruin, like a thief and robber. Like *Lucifer*, not content with his lot and ambitiously desiring higher things, he set himself in opposition against God. Finally, by an unexpected change, he was cast out of the Garden of Pleasures into this mournful and gloomy valley of misery and ignorance. The first man was left to the guidance of his own counsel (Ecclesiastes 15:14) and, of his own accord, turned from the straight path into the crooked way of misery. He greedily desired the possession of knowledge of good and evil to his own destruction, as *Hermes* <note>original: "Herms," likely referring to Hermes Trismegistus, a legendary figure associated with ancient esoteric wisdom</note> and *Moses* sufficiently demonstrate.

He was fashioned by the great *Spagyrist* <note>original: "Spacyrus," an alchemical term referring here to God as the Divine Alchemist who separates and recombines elements</note> into a physical shape, created to fill the place of the fallen angels. See the *Amphitheater* <note>original: Amphithalce; likely referring to Heinrich Khunrath's "Amphitheatrum Sapientiae Aeternae"</note> of Khunrath, which is worthy of perpetual memory.
Paul, the greatest philosopher and theologian <note>original: Divine</note>, shows three parts of man: spirit, soul, and body.
</margin>

Holy Scripture bears witness to this, even the wonderful agreement between the Creator and the creature, in whom the great Creator would show himself to be triune <note>original: Unitrine</note>—one in three, or three in one. This is also the unanimous consent of all who truly professed philosophy by the light of nature.

If by chance there are any who deny these three parts, yet they must acknowledge that man was created "from the mud of the earth" <note>original: è Limo Terra</note> by the word "FIAT" <note>Latin: "Let it be done"</note>, and that he received an eternal spirit or breath of life from the mouth of God. This is that "slime of the heavens" <note>original: Limum Calorum</note> from the Lord.

<margin>
There are two souls or two spirits in man: the mortal from the "first matter" which is the life of the body, and the immortal from God.
The spirit is the life of the soul; the spirit and soul are the life of the body.
John 14.
</margin>

The "mud of the earth" <note>original: Limus Terræ</note>, or dust of the earth, is twofold: visible and invisible. He has his body or dwelling <note>original: cottage</note> from the earth and water; but the life that dwells within is from the air and firmament of fire. **This life is the sidereal <note>original: Syderiall; meaning relating to the stars or cosmos</note> spirit, and is properly the man, not flesh and blood.** Just as the sidereal spirit is the life of the body, so the spirit of the Lord is the life of the rational <note>original: Intellectuall</note> soul.

And as the sidereal spirit dwells in the body and works therein day and night, for this invisible—

<meta>The previous page discussed how man is a microcosm of the universe, containing the properties of all elements and celestial bodies.</meta>

<margin>God created man to be His tabernacle <note>a dwelling place or sanctuary</note> in this world as well as in the world to come.</margin>
...invisible is himself the firmament and has all things in him; so the Spirit of the Lord, the **WORD** of God, the eternal man, dwells in the soul. The house is the habitation of the soul, and the soul is the habitation and cottage of God. 

Therefore, man is the most perfect completion of all God’s works, the most complete figure of the world, and the express image of God, in whom He rested from creating—having nothing more honorable before Him to be created. All the wisdom and power of the Creator is enclosed and perfected in him as the supreme masterpiece, in that he contains all things in himself that are in God. When (I say) he was made on the sixth day out of all things—the last of the creatures and an image not only of the eternal God but also of the great world <note>the macrocosm</note>—because he understands and contains all things in himself, it follows that there are three worlds or heavens in man. He is carried about by three worlds—or rather, is the whole world himself—and is a most sure and undoubted pattern of the whole universe.

<margin>And everyone is an example of God in a small image <note>original Latin: *Exemplumque Dei quisque est in imagine parva*, from the Roman poet Manilius</note>. That which is "natured" <note>created</note> takes after the nature of that which "natured" it <note>the creator</note>.</margin>

And therefore, some have called him the "Fourth World," in whom are found all those things that are in the other three; for this reason, he may also be called by the name of every creature. He has a spirit or mind from God; for what else is the spirit of man, which God breathed into him, but God Himself dwelling in us? 

<margin>God dwells in the soul as if in the heaven of man.</margin>
The invisible body or true internal man, consisting of reason and an astral spirit <note>a celestial or "star-derived" spirit thought to link the physical body and the soul</note>, agrees with the angels and is their companion. And if he is a true <term>magician</term> <note>in this context, one who understands the hidden laws of nature and the divine</note>, he is...

<meta>This page continues the discussion of man as a microcosm, or "little world," focusing on his relationship to the divine and angelic realms. The previous page likely introduced man's unique position in the hierarchy of creation.</meta>

...not inferior to the angels in all magical operation, and is lord and possessor of all things. He has his mortal physical body from the structure of the world and all things created within it, for all external things are nothing else but the body of man. So it is that he partakes of a threefold world: of the Archetype <note>original: "Archetype," referring to the divine blueprint or the mind of God</note> or God-like world in God; of the Intelligible <note>original: "Intelligible," meaning the realm perceived by the intellect rather than the senses</note> or Angelic world; and of the sensible, Elemental, or physical world. He has a symbolic operation and interaction with them all.

<margin>
The Mind, *Nous* <note>original Greek: νοῦς</note>, is the chariot driver or helmsman of the Soul or Rational Spirit, and like the eternal God, it encompasses all beings, times, and places.
</margin>

1. He communicates with God in his soul or mind because, by the breath of life, he was made in His image. The intellectual soul is a certain particle of the divine soul, in which God has sown certain seeds and resemblances of His mind within us. This is much like an echo, which sends back its voice from the resemblance of the air, by which it expresses a living soul. The mind, raised up into the most high God and united with Him, converses with God and performs the same works. There is no disposition or anything in man that does not clearly reflect something of the divinity, nor is there anything in God that is not represented in man.

2. He has a resemblance to the angels in respect to his invisible body and rational soul. By their help, he works and is preserved alongside the angels, and he possesses the same wisdom that they have; for he is <term>Gabalis socius Angelorum</term> <note>original Latin: "the curdled companion of the angels," a phrase suggesting man is a condensed or "thickened" version of the angelic nature</note>, a companion mixed into fellowship with the angels.

<summary>This page describes man as a microcosm who bridges the gap between the divine, angelic, and physical worlds, possessing a soul that reflects God and a rational spirit that connects him to the angels.</summary>
<keywords>microcosm, Archetype, Nous, Intellectual Soul, angelic realm, divinity in man, Gabalis socius Angelorum</keywords>

--- Page 321 ---
<meta>This page continues a discussion on the composition of the human being as a reflection of the universe, detailing how humans relate to the stars, the elements, and other living things.</meta>

3. He participates with the firmament and stars, from whom he received that astral body or siderial spirit <note>original: "Syderiall Spirit," referring to the celestial or star-derived part of a human being in alchemical and hermetic thought</note>, which is the true astral man. For flesh and blood is not the man, but rather the spirit contained in them. This astral spirit is the subject of human reason, which contains the senses and wisdom within it, and is made a living creature <note>original: "Animall," used here in its root sense from the Latin "anima," meaning a being endowed with life or a soul</note> together with the body. This spirit and the stars are but one thing in man, but the body is the subject of this spirit; and so the stars rule man in the spirit, and the spirit of a man rules the body in flesh and blood. This spirit is mortal; only the intellectual soul which God breathed into man is immortal.

4. He partakes of the elements, for from these he had his mortal, physical, earthy body. And because the world—which is the parent of man according to Paracelsus <note>original: "Paratelfus," referring to the influential Swiss physician and alchemist Theophrastus Paracelsus (1493–1541)</note>—has in it the four elementary spirits of the four inhabitants of the world, as also the fifth kind of "Flages" <note>original: "Flages," a term used in Paracelsian philosophy to describe certain types of spirits or spiritual entities associated with the elements or planetary influences</note> of a thousand sorts incorporated into the soul of the microcosm <term>microcosm: the idea that a human being is a "little world" that encapsulates the entire universe</term>, the imagination of these five spirits in the elements must also be in the microcosm of man. However, the use of man's reason, according to the will and command of God, acts as a chain with which those five spirits are knit and bound together, so that they may comply with his imagination.

5. He partakes of all creatures made of the elements <note>original: "Elemented Animalls"</note>, as well as vegetables and minerals; for he has in him the nature and properties of all these.

<margin>
...out of nothing, and man was made of all those things.

...[he] has the parts of the whole world, nor is there anything in the "great world" <note>The "great world" refers to the macrocosm or the universe as a whole.</note> which may not really be found in man. The Son is like the Father in all things; the Father being known, the Son is known also. Therefore man—the greatest miracle of nature and the most wonderful distillation <note>original: "Extract"</note> and essence <note>original: "kernell"</note> of the four elements, the finest workmanship of God and the most perfect model <note>original: "Samplar"</note> of the world—is truly every creature. For he is the entire world, and he alone has this honor: to bear a resemblance to all things, to interact <note>original: "operation"</note> with all things, and to have communion <note>original: "conversation"</note> with all things. Indeed, he rises to such perfection that he is made a Son of God, transformed into the same image which is God and made one with Him. This is not granted to angels, or the world, or any other creature, but to man only: that he should become the Son of God and be united to God.

<margin>
The "great world" was the womb <note>original: "matrix"</note> of *Adam*; thus the whole framework of the world is the mother of all things that are produced. 

*John 17:11; 22, 23.*
</margin>
But before we proceed any further, it is necessary that we treat here a little more extensively the <term>Sidereal</term><note>Archaic: "syderyall"; relating to the stars or celestial influences on the human spirit.</note> or invisible man—that is, his origin and power. If *Aristotle* had noticed this <term>Olympic Spirit</term><note>In Paracelsian philosophy, the "Olympic Spirit" refers to the astral or spiritual part of a human being that connects them to the heavens and the stars.</note> and *Galen* had valued it more, such errors would not have crept into philosophy and medicine <note>original: "Physick"</note>—errors originating from the heathen masters of error—to say nothing of theology <note>original: "Divinity"</note> at present.

<margin>
That which is perceptible and that which is imperceptible has a sidereal spirit or a sidereal body within it.
</margin>
The invisible man or <term>Olympic Spirit</term> is born in us in this manner. *Adam* and *Eve* did not descend from other parents as we, their posterity, do, but were taken (as has been said) from the dust of the earth <note>original: *ex LIMO TERRAE*</note>, out of the dust of the ground or the "great world," with respect to the mortal body, which is vi-

<margin>
*Eve* is *Adam* transplanted. The first humans were created; the rest proceed from the Being of the seed of Essence.
</margin>

<summary>The author describes man as a "microcosm" containing all elements of the universe and uniquely capable of divine union. He introduces the "Olympic Spirit" or "sidereal man" as the invisible, celestial component of human nature, contrasting this spiritual view with the errors of classical philosophers like Aristotle and Galen.</summary>
